Step 4 (conclusion):
Finally, we move back to the assertion regarding
(Z_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(C_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
. Observe that

-\infty<-\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\widehat{B}_{i}(\omega)\leq\lim_{k\to\infty}M_{k}(\omega)=\lim_{k\to\infty}\widehat{Z}_{k}(\omega)-\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}(\widehat{B}_{i}(\omega)-\widehat{C}_{i}(\omega))<\infty,

where
\widehat{Z}_{k}(\omega),\widehat{B}_{i}(\omega),\widehat{C}_{i}(\omega)\geq 0
implying that

\lim_{k\to\infty}\widehat{Z}_{k}(\omega)<\infty\quad\text{and}\quad\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\widehat{C}_{i}(\omega)<\infty

for almost all
\omega\in\Omega
.
Moreover, it holds true that

Z_{k}(\omega)=\widehat{Z}_{k}(\omega)\prod_{i=0}^{k-1}(1+A_{i}(\omega)),

where both
\widehat{Z}_{k}(\omega)
and
\prod_{i=0}^{k-1}(1+A_{i}(\omega))
converge for almost all
\omega\in\Omega
. The latter one follows by monotonicity and

0\leq\prod_{i=0}^{k-1}(1+A_{i}(\omega))\leq\exp(\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}A_{i}(\omega)),

where the upper bound converges by assumption. Therefore,
\lim_{k\to\infty}Z_{k}(\omega)=Z_{\infty}(\omega)
exists for almost all
\omega\in\Omega
. Similarly, we have

\sum_{i=0}^{k}C_{i}(\omega)=\sum_{i=0}^{k}\widehat{C}_{i}(\omega)\prod_{j=0}^{i}(1+A_{j}(\omega))\leq\left(\prod_{j=0}^{\infty}(1+A_{j}(\omega))\right)\sum_{i=0}^{k}\widehat{C}_{i}(\omega)

which implies

\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}C_{i}(\omega)<\infty

for almost all
\omega\in\Omega
.
∎

The conclusion of the Robbins–Siegmund theorem is qualitative: it yields almost sure convergence and the summability of the accumulated descent terms. This summability property will later be
used once more in Section
4.4
, where we refine the argument and derive almost sure convergence rates from it. For the moment, we only use it to deduce convergence to zero of suitable non-negative quantities. The following corollary is an easy, but very useful, extension of Theorem
4.2
.

{cor}

Let
(\Omega,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
be a filtered probability space,
(Z_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
,
(A_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
,
(B_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(D_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be non-negative and
\mathcal{F}
-adapted stochastic processes, such that

\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}A_{k}<\infty,\quad\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}B_{k}<\infty\quad\text{and}\quad\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}D_{k}=\infty

almost surely. Moreover, suppose

\mathbb{E}[Z_{k+1}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]\leq Z_{k}(1+A_{k}-D_{k})+B_{k}.

Then
Z_{k}
converges almost surely to
0
for
k\to\infty
.

Proof.

In the setting of Theorem
4.2
, we have
Z_{k}=Z_{k}
,
A_{k}=A_{k}
,
B_{k}=B_{k}
and
C_{k}=D_{k}Z_{k}
. By Theorem
4.2
we there exists
Z_{\infty}
which is almost surely finite, integrable and satisfies
Z_{k}\to Z_{\infty}
almost surely. In addition, we also have that
\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}C_{k}=\sum D_{k}Z_{k}<\infty
implying that
\liminf_{k\to\infty}Z_{k}=0
due to the assumption
\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}D_{k}=\infty
almost surely. Consequently, as the limit inferior and limit coincide for converging sequences, we have

Z_{\infty}=\lim_{k\to\infty}Z_{k}=\liminf_{k\to\infty}Z_{k}=0\quad\text{almost surely}\,.

∎

We are now ready to prove almost sure convergence of SGD in the non-convex setting. We will assume that the objective function
F
is
L
-smooth and lower bounded, i.e.
\inf_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\ F(x)>-\infty
. Similar to the case of gradient descent, we do not expect more than convergence to stationary points. In particular, we are able to extend Theorem
2.2.3
to the stochastic version.
The proof is based on a one-step conditional descent estimate, (
4.2
). This estimate is the stochastic analogue of the deterministic descent condition (
2.2
): although the objective value does not decrease path-wise in every iteration, it decreases after taking conditional expectations, up to an additional
error term caused by the stochastic gradient noise. This yields a recursion of the form
(
4.4
) required to apply the Robbins–Siegmund theorem. The estimate (
4.2
) is the central technical step in the analysis of SGD. It will reappear throughout the rest of this chapter.

{thm}
[SGD almost sure convergence]
Let
F:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be
L
-smooth and bounded from below by
F^{\ast}=\inf_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\ F(x)>-\infty
, let
(\alpha_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
(deterministic or
\mathcal{F}
-adapted) satisfy

\alpha_{k}>0,\quad\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{k}=\infty\quad\text{and}\quad\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{k}^{2}<\infty

(almost surely). We assume that the assumptions of Lemma
4.1.2
are satisfied, and

\mathbb{E}[\|\nabla_{x}f(x,Z)-\mathbb{E}[\nabla_{x}f(x,Z)]\|^{2}]\leq c(1+(F(x)-F^{\ast}))

for some constant
c>0
and all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Moreover, let
X_{0}
be a random variable such that
\mathbb{E}[F(X_{0})]<\infty
and
(X_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be the sequence of random variables generated by Algorithm
6
. Then it holds true that the sequence of random variables
(F(X_{k}))_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
converges almost surely to some random variable
F_{\infty}
, almost surely finite, and

\liminf_{k\to\infty}\|\nabla_{x}F(X_{k})\|^{2}=0

almost surely.

Proof.

We define the natural filtration
\mathcal{F}=(\mathcal{F}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
through
\mathcal{F}_{k}=\sigma(X_{m},m\leq k)=\sigma(X_{0},\ Z_{m},m\leq k)
and note that
(\alpha_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is
\mathcal{F}
-adapted per construction. Using the
L
-smoothness of
F
we obtain (path-wise) that

\displaystyle F(X_{k+1})

\displaystyle=F(X_{k}-\alpha_{k}\nabla_{x}f(X_{k},Z_{k+1}))

\displaystyle\leq F(X_{k})-\alpha_{k}\langle\nabla_{x}F(X_{k}),\nabla_{x}f(X_{k},Z_{k+1})\rangle+\alpha_{k}^{2}\frac{L}{2}\|\nabla_{x}f(X_{k},Z_{k+1})\|^{2}

\displaystyle=F(X_{k})-\alpha_{k}\|\nabla_{x}F(X_{k})\|^{2}+\alpha_{k}\langle\nabla_{x}F(X_{k}),M_{k+1}\rangle

\displaystyle\quad+\alpha_{k}^{2}\frac{L}{2}(\|\nabla_{x}F(X_{k})\|^{2}-2\langle\nabla_{x}F(X_{k}),M_{k+1}\rangle+\|M_{k+1}\|^{2}),

where
M_{k+1}:=\nabla_{x}F(X_{k})-\nabla_{x}f(X_{k},Z_{k+1})
. By Lemma
4.1.2
and Lemma
4.1.3
we obtain

\mathbb{E}[M_{k+1}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]=0

and

\mathbb{E}[\|M_{k+1}\|^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]\leq c(1+(F(X_{k})-F^{\ast})).

This yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[F(X_{k+1})-F^{\ast}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]

\displaystyle\leq(F(X_{k})-F^{\ast})+(\frac{L}{2}\alpha_{k}^{2}-\alpha_{k})\|\nabla_{x}F(X_{k})\|^{2}+\frac{L}{2}\alpha_{k}^{2}c(1+(F(X_{k})-F^{\ast}))

\displaystyle=(1+c\frac{L}{2}\alpha_{k}^{2})(F(X_{k})-F^{\ast})+c\frac{L}{2}\alpha_{k}^{2}-\alpha_{k}(1-\frac{L}{2}\alpha_{k})\|\nabla_{x}F(X_{k})\|^{2}.

(4.6)

W.l.o.g. we assume that
\alpha_{k}\leq(1-\varepsilon)\frac{2}{L}
for some
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
(else let
k
be sufficiently large), such that

(1-\frac{L}{2}\alpha_{k})\geq\varepsilon>0
. We can now apply Theorem
4.2
to imply that
\lim_{k\to\infty}F(X_{k})-F^{\ast}
exists almost surely and is finite, as well as

\varepsilon\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{k}\|\nabla_{x}F(X_{k})\|^{2}\leq\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{k}(1-\frac{L}{2}\alpha_{k})\|\nabla_{x}F(X_{k})\|^{2}<\infty

almost surely. Since we have assumed
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{k}=\infty
almost surely,
we obtain

\liminf_{k\to\infty}\|\nabla_{x}F(X_{k})\|^{2}=0

almost surely.
∎

Remark
\thethm
.

In the literature of SGD, the so-called ABC-condition has been established as a popular assumption on the variance of the stochastic gradient estimators. The assumption is an important relaxation of bounded noise and can be verified in many applications
[
16
,
23
]
. Here, we assume that there exist constants
A,B,C\geq 0
such that

\mathbb{E}[\|\nabla_{x}f(x,Z)-\mathbb{E}[\nabla_{x}f(x,Z)]\|^{2}]\leq A(F(x)-F^{\ast})+B\|\nabla_{x}F(x)\|^{2}+C\,.

All the results, presented in these lecture notes based on bounded variance assumptions can be extended to the ABC-condition.

Before delving into the derivation of convergence rates for SGD, we formulate the following Corollary which states almost sure convergence under same assumptions of Theorem
4.2
, but with the additional property of strong convexity of
F
.

{cor}

Suppose that the assumptions of Theorem
4.2
are satisfied and additionally, assume that
F
is
\mu
-strongly convex. Then the sequence of random variables
(X_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
converges almost surely to the unique global minimum
x_{\ast}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
of
F
.

Proof.

Let
F
be
\mu
-strongly convex and denote
x_{\ast}=\arg\min_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\ F(x)
. Following Proposition
, we have that

\frac{1}{2}\|\nabla F(x)\|^{2}\geq\mu(F(x)-F(x_{\ast}))\,.

W.l.o.g. we assume this time that that
\alpha_{k}\leq\frac{1}{L}
so that from (
4.2
) we have that

\mathbb{E}[F(X_{k+1})-F(x_{\ast})\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]\leq(1+c\frac{L}{2}\alpha_{k}^{2}-\alpha_{k}\mu)(F(X_{k})-F(x_{\ast}))+c\frac{L}{2}\alpha_{k}^{2}\,,

where we have used that
(1-\frac{L}{2}\alpha_{k})\geq\frac{1}{2}
. By Robbins-Siegmund (Corollary
4.2
) we deduce that
F(X_{k})-F(x_{\ast})\to 0
almost surely as
k\to\infty
. Again by strong convexity we recall that
\frac{\mu}{2}\|X_{k}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}\leq F(X_{k})-F(x_{\ast})
(almost surely) which yields the claim.
∎

4.3
Convergence in expectation

Similarly as in the setting of the deterministic gradient descent scheme, the previous result states convergence to a stationary point without explicit rate of convergence, but under rather mild assumptions on the objective function
F
. In order to obtain a speed of convergence, additional properties such as convexity must be assumed for
F
. We will observe that the convergence behavior is worse than in the setting of deterministic gradient descent methods. However, in order to make a fair comparison between deterministic and stochastic gradient descent schemes, one must consider the complexity of both algorithms, including the computational cost of implementation.
In the following section, we consider a row of convergence results in expectation.

4.3.1
Convergence for non-convex and smooth objective functions

We begin with the convergence of the expected gradient norm under a non-convex but smooth setting. The proof builds up on the estimates derived in the proof of Theorem
4.2
.

{thm}

Suppose that the assumptions of Theorem
4.2
and the additional assumption that
\alpha_{k}\in(0,1/L]
almost surely are in place. Then for all
K>0
it holds true that

\min_{0\leq k\leq K}\mathbb{E}[\|\nabla F(X_{k})\|^{2}]\leq\frac{2(\mathbb{E}[F(X_{0})]-F^{\ast})}{\sum_{j=0}^{K}\alpha_{j}}+\frac{cL\sum_{k=0}^{K}\alpha_{k}^{2}}{\sum_{j=0}^{K}\alpha_{j}}\,.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{F}_{k}=\sigma(X_{m},m\leq k)
be the natural filtration and consider the one-step conditional descent condition (
4.2
). We take another expectation on both sides which yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[F(X_{k+1})]=\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{E}[F(X_{k+1})\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]]\leq\mathbb{E}[F(X_{k})]-\alpha_{k}(1-\frac{L\alpha_{k}}{2})\mathbb{E}[\|\nabla_{x}F(X_{k})\|^{2}]+c\frac{L}{2}\alpha_{k}^{2}.

Reordering the inequality and iterating over
k=0,\dots,K
gives

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{K}\alpha_{k}(1-\frac{L\alpha_{k}}{2})\mathbb{E}[\|\nabla_{x}F(X_{k})\|^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{K}\Big(\mathbb{E}[F(X_{k})]-\mathbb{E}[F(X_{k+1})]\Big)+c\frac{L}{2}\sum_{k=0}^{K}\alpha_{k}^{2}

\displaystyle=(\mathbb{E}[F(X_{0})]-\mathbb{E}[F(X_{K+1})])+c\frac{L}{2}\sum_{k=0}^{K}\alpha_{k}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq(\mathbb{E}[F(X_{0})]-F^{\ast})+c\frac{L}{2}\sum_{k=0}^{K}\alpha_{k}^{2}

Under the assumption
\alpha_{k}\leq 1/L
for all
k\geq 0
, we have that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=0}^{K}\alpha_{k}\mathbb{E}[\|\nabla_{x}F(X_{k})\|^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq(\mathbb{E}[F(X_{0})]-F^{\ast})+c\frac{L}{2}\sum_{k=0}^{K}\alpha_{k}^{2}\,.

Similar to the proof of Corollary
2.2.3
, we have

\min_{0\leq k\leq K}\mathbb{E}[\|\nabla F(X_{k})\|^{2}]\leq\frac{1}{\sum_{j=0}^{K}\alpha_{j}}\sum_{k=0}^{K}\alpha_{k}\mathbb{E}[\|\nabla_{x}F(X_{k})\|^{2}]\leq\frac{2(\mathbb{E}[F(X_{0})]-F^{\ast})}{\sum_{j=0}^{K}\alpha_{j}}+\frac{cL\sum_{k=0}^{K}\alpha_{k}^{2}}{\sum_{j=0}^{K}\alpha_{j}}\,.

∎

From the derived upper bound we obtain convergence under the sufficient condition that

\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{k}=\infty\quad\text{and}\quad\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{k}^{2}<\infty.

In order to quantify the speed of convergence, we provide a specific choice of the step sizes. Note that in the case below, we have
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{k}^{2}=\infty
but

\frac{\sum_{k=0}^{K}\alpha_{k}^{2}}{\sum_{k=0}^{K}\alpha_{k}}\to 0\,.

{cor}

Suppose that the same conditions of Theorem
4.3.1
are satisfied. Moreover, let
\alpha_{k}:=\frac{1}{L\sqrt{k+1}}
. Then it holds true that

\min_{0\leq k\leq K}\mathbb{E}[\|\nabla F(X_{k})\|^{2}]\in\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\log(K)}{\sqrt{K}}\right).

Proof.

Firstly, we observe that

\sum_{j=0}^{K}\alpha_{j}=\frac{1}{L}\sum_{j=0}^{K}\frac{1}{\sqrt{j+1}}\geq\frac{1}{L}\int_{1}^{K}\frac{1}{\sqrt{t+1}}\,{\mathrm{d}}t=\frac{2}{L}(\sqrt{K+1}-\sqrt{2})\geq\frac{1}{L}\sqrt{K+1},

for sufficiently large
K
(
K\geq 7
). On the other side, we have

\sum_{j=0}^{K}\alpha_{j}^{2}=\frac{1}{L^{2}}\sum_{j=0}^{K}\frac{1}{j+1}\leq\frac{1}{L^{2}}\left(1+\int_{0}^{K}\frac{1}{t+1}\,{\mathrm{d}}t\right)=\frac{1}{L^{2}}(1+\log(K+1)).

By the upper bound derived in Theorem
4.3.1
, we obtain

\min_{0\leq k\leq K}\mathbb{E}[\|\nabla F(X_{k})\|^{2}]\leq\frac{2L(\mathbb{E}[F(X_{0})]-F^{\ast})+\frac{c}{L}(1+\alpha_{0}L)}{\sqrt{K+1}}+\frac{c}{L}(1+\alpha_{0}L)\frac{\log(K+1)}{\sqrt{K+1}}\in\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\log(K)}{\sqrt{K}}\right)\,.

∎

4.3.2
Convergence for convex and smooth objective functions

In the following, we will prove convergence of SGD under the assumption that the objective function is convex. Compared to the convergence result for gradient descent schemes, we will prove a slower convergence behavior. The following Theorem presents the resulting error bound in expectation.

{thm}

[SGD for convex and smooth objective function]
Let
F:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be convex and
L
-smooth, and assume that the set of global minima of
F
is non-empty. We assume that the assumptions of Lemma
4.1.2
are satisfied and that there exists
c>0
such that

\mathbb{E}[\|\nabla_{x}f(x,Z)-\mathbb{E}[\nabla_{x}f(x,Z)]\|^{2}]\leq c

for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Let
X_{0}
be a random variable such that
\mathbb{E}[|F(X_{0})|+\|X_{0}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}]<\infty
for some
x_{\ast}\in\arg\min_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\ F(x)
. Moreover, let
(X_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be generated by Algorithm
6
with deterministic and decreasing sequence of step sizes
(\alpha_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
\alpha_{k}\in(0,\frac{1}{L}]
. Then for

\bar{X}_{K}:=\sum_{k=0}^{K-1}w_{k}^{K}X_{k+1},\quad w_{k}^{K}:=\frac{\alpha_{k}}{\sum_{j=0}^{K-1}\alpha_{j}},\quad K\geq 2,

it holds true that

\mathbb{E}[F(\bar{X}_{K})-F(x_{\ast})]\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}[\|X_{0}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}]}{2\sum_{j=0}^{K-1}\alpha_{j}}+\frac{c(1+\alpha_{0}L)\sum_{k=0}^{K-1}\alpha_{k}^{2}}{2\sum_{j=0}^{K-1}\alpha_{j}}.

Proof.

Let
x_{\ast}\in\arg\min_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\ F(x)
and
\mathcal{F}_{k}=\sigma(X_{m},m\leq k)
be the natural filtration. Similar as in the proof of Theorem
4.2
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[F(X_{k+1})]=\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{E}[F(X_{k+1})\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]]\leq\mathbb{E}[F(X_{k})]-\alpha_{k}(1-\frac{L\alpha_{k}}{2})\mathbb{E}[\|\nabla_{x}F(X_{k})\|^{2}]+c\frac{L}{2}\alpha_{k}^{2}.

We can also derive the following,

\displaystyle\|X_{k+1}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\|X_{k}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}-2\alpha_{k}\langle\nabla_{x}f(X_{k},Z_{k+1}),X_{k}-x_{\ast}\rangle+\alpha_{k}^{2}\|\nabla_{x}f(X_{k},Z_{k+1})\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\|X_{k}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}-2\alpha_{k}\langle\nabla_{x}F(X_{k}),X_{k}-x_{\ast}\rangle+\alpha_{k}^{2}\|\nabla_{x}F(X_{k})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+2\alpha_{k}\langle M_{k+1},X_{k}-x_{\ast}\rangle+\alpha_{k}^{2}\|M_{k+1}\|^{2}-2\alpha_{k}^{2}\langle M_{k+1},\nabla_{x}F(X_{k})\rangle,

where again
M_{k+1}=\nabla_{x}F(X_{k})-\nabla_{x}f(X_{k},Z_{k+1})
. Taking the conditional expectation wrt.
\mathcal{F}_{k}
results in the bound

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\|X_{k+1}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]

\displaystyle=\|X_{k}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}-2\alpha_{k}\langle\nabla_{x}F(X_{k}),X_{k}-x_{\ast}\rangle+\alpha_{k}^{2}\|\nabla_{x}F(X_{k})\|^{2}+\alpha_{k}^{2}\mathbb{E}[\|M_{k+1}\|^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]

\displaystyle\leq\|X_{k}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}-2\alpha_{k}\langle\nabla_{x}F(X_{k}),X_{k}-x_{\ast}\rangle+\alpha_{k}^{2}\|\nabla_{x}F(X_{k})\|^{2}+\alpha_{k}^{2}c\,.

We take again expectation and rewrite the derived inequality in form

\displaystyle 2\alpha_{k}\mathbb{E}[\langle\nabla_{x}F(X_{k}),X_{k}-x_{\ast}\rangle]\leq\mathbb{E}[\|X_{k}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}]-\mathbb{E}[\|X_{k+1}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}]+\alpha_{k}^{2}\mathbb{E}[\|\nabla_{x}F(X_{k})\|^{2}]+\alpha_{k}^{2}c\,.

By convexity of
F
we have almost surely that

F(X_{k})\leq F(x_{\ast})+\langle X_{k}-x_{\ast},\nabla_{x}F(X_{k})\rangle,

such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[F(X_{k+1})]

\displaystyle\leq F(x_{\ast})+\mathbb{E}[\langle X_{k}-x_{\ast},\nabla_{x}F(X_{k})\rangle]-\alpha_{k}(1-\frac{L\alpha_{k}}{2})\mathbb{E}[\|\nabla_{x}F(X_{k})\|^{2}]+c\frac{L}{2}\alpha_{k}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq F(x_{\ast})+\frac{1}{2\alpha_{k}}\left(\mathbb{E}[\|X_{k}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}]-\mathbb{E}[\|X_{k+1}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}]\right)

\displaystyle\quad-\alpha_{k}\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{L\alpha_{k}}{2}\right)\mathbb{E}[\|\nabla_{x}F(X_{k})\|^{2}]+(\frac{\alpha_{k}}{2}+\frac{L\alpha_{k}^{2}}{2})c

\displaystyle\leq F(x_{\ast})+\frac{1}{2\alpha_{k}}\left(\mathbb{E}[\|X_{k}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}]-\mathbb{E}[\|X_{k+1}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}]\right)+\left(\frac{\alpha_{k}}{2}+\frac{L\alpha_{k}^{2}}{2}\right)c,

where we have used that
(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{L\alpha_{k}}{2})\geq 0
. Note that
\sum_{k=0}^{K-1}w_{k}^{K}=1
, such that by Jensen’s inequality it follows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[F(\bar{X}_{K})-F(x_{\ast})]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\sum_{j=0}^{K-1}\alpha_{j}}\sum_{k=0}^{K-1}\alpha_{k}\mathbb{E}[F(X_{k+1})-F(x_{\ast})]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2\sum_{j=0}^{K-1}\alpha_{j}}\sum_{k=0}^{K-1}\left(\mathbb{E}[\|X_{k}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}]-\mathbb{E}[\|X_{k+1}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}]\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{\sum_{j=0}^{K-1}\alpha_{j}}\sum_{k=0}^{K-1}\left(\frac{\alpha_{k}^{2}}{2}+\frac{\alpha_{k}^{3}L}{2}\right)c

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}[\|X_{0}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}]}{2\sum_{j=0}^{K-1}\alpha_{j}}+\frac{c(1+\alpha_{0}L)\sum_{k=0}^{K-1}\alpha_{k}^{2}}{2\sum_{j=0}^{K-1}\alpha_{j}},

where we have used that
\alpha_{k}
is decreasing and therefore,
\alpha_{k}\leq\alpha_{0}
.
∎

Remark
\thethm
.

The convergence statement in Theorem
4.3.2
is formulated for the weighted average

\bar{X}_{K}

rather than for the last iterate
X_{K}
. This is one of the main differences compared to the
deterministic gradient descent proof of Theorem
2.2.4
. In the deterministic convex setting with a
fixed step size, the descent estimate leads to a simple telescoping sum and, since the objective values decrease monotonically, one directly obtains a last-iterate rate. For SGD, the objective values do not decrease monotonically along individual trajectories because of the stochastic gradient noise. After taking expectations, the one-step estimate still gives a telescoping bound, but only for the weighted sum

\sum_{k=0}^{N}\alpha_{k}\mathbb{E}\big[F(X_{k})-F(x^{\ast})\big].

To convert this bound into a convergence rate for objective values, we therefore introduced the weighted average
\bar{X}_{K}
and used convexity of
F
together with Jensen’s inequality.

Similarly as in the previous non-convex setting, we obtain the following rate of convergence.

{cor}

Suppose that the same conditions of Theorem
4.3.2
are satisfied. Moreover, let
\alpha_{k}:=\frac{1}{L\sqrt{k+1}}
. Then it holds true that

\mathbb{E}[F(\bar{X}_{K})-F(x_{\ast})]\in\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\log(K)}{\sqrt{K}}\right).

Proof.

Recall from Corollary
4.3.1
that

\sum_{j=0}^{K-1}\alpha_{j}\geq\frac{1}{L}\sqrt{K},

for sufficiently large
K
(
K\geq 8
) and

\sum_{j=0}^{K-1}\alpha_{j}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{L^{2}}(1+\log(K)).

By the upper bound derived in Theorem
4.3.2
, we obtain

\mathbb{E}[F(\bar{X}_{K})-F(x_{\ast})]\leq\frac{L\mathbb{E}[\|X_{0}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}]+\frac{c}{2L}(1+\alpha_{0}L)}{\sqrt{K}}+\frac{c}{2L}(1+\alpha_{0}L)\frac{\log(K)}{\sqrt{K}}\in\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\log(K)}{\sqrt{K}}\right)\,.

∎

4.3.3
Convergence for strongly convex and smooth objective functions

If we additionally assume strong convexity, we can further improve the derived upper bound. Moreover, we can even prove convergence to a unique global minimum of
F
, as we also did for the deterministic gradient descent scheme. However, due to the stochastic approximation of the gradient, we lose the behavior of linear convergence.

{thm}

[SGD for strong convex and smooth objective function]
Let
F:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be
\mu
-strongly convex and
L
-smooth. We assume that the assumptions of Lemma
4.1.2
are satisfied and that there exists
c>0
such that

\mathbb{E}[\|\nabla_{x}f(x,Z)-\mathbb{E}[\nabla_{x}f(x,Z)]\|^{2}]\leq c

for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Let
X_{0}
be a random variable such that
\mathbb{E}[|F(X_{0})|+\|X_{0}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}]<\infty
, where
x_{\ast}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is the unique global minimum of
F
. Moreover, let
(X_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be generated by Algorithm
6
with deterministic and decreasing sequence of step sizes
(\alpha_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
\alpha_{k}\in(0,\frac{1}{L}]
. Then it holds true that

\mathbb{E}[\|X_{k+1}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}]\leq(1-\alpha_{k}\mu)\mathbb{E}[\|X_{k}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}]+c\alpha_{k}^{2}

for all
k\geq 0
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{F}_{k}=\sigma(X_{m},m\leq k)
be again the natural filtration and recall that we have derived in the proof of Theorem
4.3.2
that

\mathbb{E}[\|X_{k+1}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}]\leq\mathbb{E}[\|X_{k}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}]-2\alpha_{k}\mathbb{E}[\langle\nabla_{x}F(X_{k}),X_{k}-x_{\ast}\rangle]+\alpha_{k}^{2}\mathbb{E}[\|\nabla_{x}F(X_{k})\|^{2}]+\alpha_{k}^{2}c.

By
\mu
-strong convexity we have that

F(x_{\ast})-F(X_{k})\geq\langle x_{\ast}-X_{k},\nabla_{x}F(X_{k})\rangle+\frac{\mu}{2}\|X_{k}-x_{\ast}\|^{2},

which can be rewritten as

-\langle X_{k}-x_{\ast},\nabla_{x}F(X_{k})\rangle\leq-\left(F(X_{k})-F(x_{\ast})\right)-\frac{\mu}{2}\|X_{k}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}\quad\text{almost surely}\,.

Combining both inequalities, we obtain that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\|X_{k+1}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}]\leq(1-\alpha_{k}\mu)\mathbb{E}[\|X_{k}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}]-2\alpha_{k}\mathbb{E}[F(X_{k})-F(x_{\ast})]+\alpha_{k}^{2}\mathbb{E}[\|\nabla_{x}F(X_{k})\|^{2}]+\alpha_{k}^{2}c\,.

The assumption of
L
-smoothness implies that

-(F(X_{k})-F(x_{\ast}))\leq-\frac{1}{2L}\|\nabla_{x}F(X_{k})\|^{2}\quad\text{almost surely},

such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\|X_{k+1}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq(1-\alpha_{k}\mu)\mathbb{E}[\|X_{k}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}]+\alpha_{k}(\alpha_{k}-\frac{1}{L})\mathbb{E}[\|\nabla_{x}F(X_{k})\|^{2}]+\alpha_{k}^{2}c

\displaystyle\leq(1-\alpha_{k}\mu)\mathbb{E}[\|X_{k}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}]+\alpha_{k}^{2}c,

where we have used that
\alpha_{k}\leq\frac{1}{L}
.
∎

From the above derived error bound we observe that the iterated error decomposes into the error arising due to the optimization error from the exact gradient descent scheme applied to strongly convex and smooth objective functions, and into an error arising from the variance of the stochastic approximation of the gradients. In order to deduce a convergence rate along the iterations, we need to balance both errors by either decreasing the step size
\alpha_{k}
sufficiently or by decreasing the variance term. The latter one will be the topic of Section
4.5
, where we consider methods of variance reduction. In the following, we will present the former approach of decreasing the step size to
0
.

{cor}

Suppose that the same conditions as in Theorem
4.3.3
are satisfied. Moreover, let
\alpha_{k}=\frac{\tau}{\mu(k+s)}
for some
\tau\geq 2
and
s:=\kappa\tau=\frac{L}{\mu}\tau\geq 1
. Then it holds true that
\alpha_{0}\leq\frac{1}{L}
and there exists
\gamma\geq 2(s+1)\max(\mathbb{E}[\|X_{0}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}],\frac{\tau^{2}c}{s\mu^{2}})
such that

\mathbb{E}[\|X_{k}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}]\leq\frac{\gamma}{k+s}

for all
k\geq 1
.

Proof.

Firstly, we observe that by definition

\alpha_{0}=\frac{\tau}{\mu\cdot s}=\frac{\tau\mu}{\mu L\tau}=\frac{1}{L}\,,

such that
\alpha_{k}\leq\frac{1}{L}
for all
k\geq 0
. We define
\Delta_{k}=\mathbb{E}[\|X_{k}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}]
and prove the second claim via induction. For
k=1
it holds true that

\displaystyle\Delta_{1}\leq(1-\alpha_{0}\mu)\Delta_{0}+\alpha_{0}^{2}c

\displaystyle\leq(1-\frac{1}{\kappa})\Delta_{0}+\frac{\tau^{2}c}{\mu^{2}s}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(s+1)2\max(\Delta_{0},\frac{\tau^{2}c}{\mu^{2}s})}{s+1}\leq\frac{\gamma}{s+1}\,.

Now, suppose that the upper bound
\Delta_{k}\leq\frac{\gamma}{k+s}
is satisfied for some
k\geq 1
, then it follows that

\displaystyle\Delta_{k+1}\leq(1-\alpha_{k}\mu)\Delta_{k}+\alpha_{k}^{2}c

\displaystyle\leq\left(1-\frac{\tau}{k+s}\right)\frac{\gamma}{k+s}+\frac{\tau^{2}c}{\mu^{2}}\frac{1}{(k+s)^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\gamma}{k+1+s}+\frac{\gamma}{(k+s)(k+1+s)}-\frac{\gamma\tau}{(k+s)^{2}}+\frac{\tau^{2}c}{\mu^{2}}\frac{1}{(k+s)^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\gamma}{k+1+s}+\frac{\gamma-\tau\gamma+\frac{\tau^{2}c}{\mu^{2}}}{(k+s)^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\gamma}{k+1+s}+\frac{\frac{\tau^{2}c}{\mu^{2}}-\gamma}{(k+s)^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\gamma}{k+1+s},

where we have used that
\gamma(1-\tau)\leq-\gamma
and
\gamma\geq 2(s+1)\max(\Delta_{0},\frac{\tau^{2}c}{\mu^{2}s})\geq\frac{\tau^{2}c}{\mu^{2}}
.
∎

4.3.4
Convergence under PL-condition and smooth objective functions

Similar to the deterministic gradient descent method, we are able to prove convergence under the PL-condition. We obtain the same type of convergence behavior as in the strong convex setting, with the main difference being the error discrepancy in the objective function evaluation.

{thm}

[SGD under PL-condition]
Let
F:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be
L
-smooth and assume that
F
satisfies the PL-condition

\|\nabla_{x}F(x)\|^{2}\geq 2r(F(x)-F^{\ast})

for some
r\in(0,L)
and all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, where
F^{\ast}=\inf_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\ F(x)>-\infty
. We assume that the assumptions of Lemma
4.1.2
are satisfied and that there exists
c>0
such that

\mathbb{E}[\|\nabla_{x}f(x,Z)-\mathbb{E}[\nabla_{x}f(x,Z)]\|^{2}]\leq c

for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Let
X_{0}
be a random variable such that
\mathbb{E}[|F(X_{0})|+\|X_{0}\|^{2}]<\infty
, and let
(X_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be generated by Algorithm
6
with deterministic and decreasing sequence of step sizes
(\alpha_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
\alpha_{k}\in(0,\frac{1}{L}]
. Then it holds true that

\mathbb{E}[F(X_{k})-F^{\ast}]\leq(1-\alpha_{k}r)\mathbb{E}[F(X_{k})-F^{\ast}]+c\frac{L}{2}\alpha_{k}^{2}\,.

Proof.

We have already seen that under
L
-smoothness we obtain the following bound

\mathbb{E}[F(X_{k+1})-F^{\ast}]\leq\mathbb{E}[F(X_{k})-F^{\ast}]-\alpha_{k}(1-\frac{L}{2}\alpha_{k})\mathbb{E}[\|\nabla_{x}F(X_{k})\|^{2}]+c\frac{L}{2}\alpha_{k}^{2}\,.

Under the PL-condition and the fact that
1-\frac{L}{2}\alpha_{k}\geq\frac{1}{2}>0
, we improve the upper bound to

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[F(X_{k+1})-F^{\ast}]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}[F(X_{k})-F^{\ast}]-\alpha_{k}(1-\frac{L}{2}\alpha_{k})2r\mathbb{E}[F(X_{k})-F^{\ast}]+c\frac{L}{2}\alpha_{k}^{2}

\displaystyle=(1-\alpha_{k}(1-\frac{L}{2}\alpha_{k})2r)\mathbb{E}[F(X_{k})-F^{\ast}]+c\frac{L}{2}\alpha_{k}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq(1-\alpha_{k}r)\mathbb{E}[F(X_{k})-F^{\ast}]+c\frac{L}{2}\alpha_{k}^{2}\,.

∎

We can apply the same step size strategy as in the strongly convex setting to derive convergence.

{cor}

Suppose that the same conditions as in Theorem
4.3.4
are satisfied. Moreover, let
\alpha_{k}=\frac{\tau}{r(k+s)}
for some
\tau\geq 2
and
s:=\frac{L}{r}\tau\geq 1
. Then it holds true that
\alpha_{0}\leq\frac{1}{L}
and there exists
\gamma\geq(s+1)2\max(\mathbb{E}[F(X_{0})-F^{\ast}],\frac{\tau^{2}c}{r^{2}})
such that

\mathbb{E}[F(X_{k})-F^{\ast}]\leq\frac{\gamma}{k+s}

for all
k\geq 1
.

Proof.

The proof proceeds line by line as the proof of Corollary
4.3.3
.
∎

4.3.5
Discussion about the complexity of SGD

In the previous sections, we derived convergence rates of SGD under non-convexity, convexity, strong convexity and the PL-condition. We obtained similar results for the deterministic GD scheme. Comparing both GD and SGD, we observe that the derived results for SGD are significantly worse.

convex

strongly convex

PL

non-convex

QoI

F(\bar{X}_{k})-F^{\ast}

\|X_{k}-X_{\ast}\|

F(X_{k})-F^{\ast}

\min_{0\leq n\leq k}\ \mathbb{E}[\|\nabla F(X_{n})\|^{2}]

GD

\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{k}\right)

\mathcal{O}(\rho^{k}),\ \rho\in(0,1)

\mathcal{O}(\rho^{k}),\ \rho\in(0,1)

\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{k}\right)

SGD

\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\log(k)}{\sqrt{k}}\right)

\mathcal{O}(\frac{1}{k})

\mathcal{O}(\frac{1}{k})

\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{k}}\right)

However, the implementation of GD might be expensive (e.g. for large data sets in empirical risk minimization) or even impossible. In this case, it is infeasible to implement GD and there is no other choice to work with SGD.

The comparison of GD and SGD gets more involved in cases where we are able to compute the exact gradient. Let us consider the empirical risk minimization problem

\min_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\ F_{N}(x),\quad F_{n}(x)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}f(x,z^{(i)}),\ z^{(i)}\in\mathbb{R}^{p},\ i=1,\dots,N,

where we assume that
x\mapsto f(x,z^{(i)})
are
\mu
-strongly convex and
L
-smooth for all
i
. We have seen that GD with a fixed step size
\bar{\alpha}\leq\frac{1}{L}
converges linearly with rate
\rho\in(0,1)
such that

\|x_{k,N}^{\mathrm{GD}}-x_{N}\|^{2}\leq\rho^{k}\|x_{0}-x_{N}\|^{2},

where
(x_{k,N}^{\mathrm{GD}})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
denotes the iteration generated by GD and
x_{N}=\arg\min_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\ F_{N}(x)
. In comparison, SGD (with decreasing step size) converges sub-linear with

\mathbb{E}[\|X_{k,N}^{\mathrm{SGD}}-x_{N}\|^{2}]\leq\frac{\gamma}{k+s}.

In order to achieve an error of a certain tolerance
\varepsilon>0
we need to iterate

(i)

k^{\mathrm{GD}}\geq\mathcal{O}(\log(\varepsilon^{-1}))
, such that
\|x_{k,N}^{\mathrm{GD}}-x_{N}\|^{2}\leq\varepsilon
,

(ii)

k^{\mathrm{SGD}}\geq\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{-1})
, such that
\mathbb{E}[\|X_{k,N}^{\mathrm{SGD}}-x_{N}\|^{2}]\leq\varepsilon
.

The first guess is, that as long we are able to compute the full gradient, there is no reason to implement SGD over GD. However, this train of thought is too naive. The reason is, that up to now we have ignored the empirical error which occurs through solving
x_{N}=\arg\min\ F_{N}(x)
. Indeed,
x_{N}
should be treated as random variable
X_{N}
, which depends on
Z^{(1)},\dots,Z^{(N)}
. We want to quantify the error of both GD and SGD to
x_{\ast}=\arg\min_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\ F(x)
, where
F(x)=\mathbb{E}[f(x,Z)]
is the expected risk. For SGD, implemented through Algorithm
6
, we again obtain convergence (with decreasing step size) in the form of

\mathbb{E}[\|X_{k,N}^{\mathrm{SGD}}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}]\leq\frac{\gamma}{k+s}.

However, the situation changes for GD. Assuming that we are not able to compute the exact gradient of
F
, we firstly have to approximate
F
through
F_{N}
and then apply GD to find
X_{N}=\arg\min_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\ F_{N}(x)
. The final error decomposes to

\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[\|X_{k,N}^{\mathrm{GD}}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}]\leq\mathbb{E}[\|X_{k,N}^{\mathrm{GD}}-X_{N}\|^{2}]+\mathbb{E}[\|X_{N}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}]\leq\rho^{k}\mathbb{E}[\|X_{0,N}^{\mathrm{GD}}-X_{N}\|^{2}]+\mathbb{E}[\|X_{N}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}],

where
X_{N}
denotes the random minimum of
F_{N}
given
Z^{(1)},\dots,Z^{(N)}
. In the following, we study the error
\mathbb{E}[\|X_{N}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}]
for strongly convex objective functions depending on the number of data points.

{thm}

Let
F:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be
\mu
-strongly convex and let
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{p}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
x\mapsto f(x,z)
is
\mu
-strongly convex for all
z\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
. Moreover, let
x_{\ast}=\arg\min_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\ F(x)
, and assume that
\mathbb{E}[\nabla_{x}f(x_{\ast},Z)]=\nabla_{x}F(x_{\ast})
and

\mathbb{E}[\|\nabla_{x}f(x_{\ast},Z)-\mathbb{E}[\nabla_{x}f(x_{\ast},Z)]\|^{2}]\leq B

for some
B>0
. Let
Z^{(1)},\dots,Z^{(N)}
be a family of i.i.d. random variables with distribution
\mu_{Z}
, then it holds true that

\mathbb{E}[\|X_{N}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}]\leq\frac{B}{\mu^{2}}\frac{1}{N},

where
X_{N}=\arg\min_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}F_{N}(x)
.

Proof.

Let

\displaystyle x_{\ast}

\displaystyle=\arg\min_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\ F(x),\quad F(x)=\mathbb{E}[f(x,Z)]

\displaystyle X_{N}

\displaystyle=\arg\min_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\ F_{N}(x),\quad F_{N}(x)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}f(x,Z^{(i)}),

which means that
\nabla_{x}F(x_{\ast})=0
and
\nabla_{x}F_{N}(X_{N})=0
almost surely. By strong convexity we can apply Lemma
2.2.5
to imply

\displaystyle\|X_{N}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}\leq\frac{1}{\mu}\langle X_{n}-x_{\ast},\nabla_{x}F_{N}(X_{N})-\nabla_{x}F_{N}(x_{\ast})\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mu}\langle X_{n}-x_{\ast},\nabla_{x}F(x_{\ast})-\nabla_{x}F_{N}(x_{\ast})\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\mu}\|X_{N}-x_{\ast}\|\|\nabla_{x}F(x_{\ast})-\nabla_{x}F_{N}(x_{\ast})\|,

almost surely, where we have used Cauchy-Schwarz inequality in the last line. Reordering the above inequality leads to

\|X_{N}-x_{\ast}\|\leq\frac{1}{\mu}\|\nabla_{x}F(x_{\ast})-\nabla_{x}F_{N}(x_{\ast})\|

(4.7)

almost surely. Note that

\mathbb{E}[\nabla_{x}F_{N}(x_{\ast})]=\mathbb{E}[\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\nabla_{x}f(x_{\ast},Z^{(i)})]=\nabla_{x}F(x_{\ast})

such that we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\|\nabla_{x}F_{N}(x_{\ast})

\displaystyle-\nabla_{x}F(x_{\ast})\|^{2}]=\mathbb{E}[\|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(\nabla_{x}f(x_{\ast},Z^{(i)})-\mathbb{E}[\nabla_{x}f(x_{\ast},Z^{(i)})]\right)\|^{2}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}[\langle\nabla_{x}f(x_{\ast},Z^{(i)})-\mathbb{E}[\nabla_{x}f(x_{\ast},Z^{(i)})],\nabla_{x}f(x_{\ast},Z^{(j)})-\mathbb{E}[\nabla_{x}f(x_{\ast},Z^{(j)})]\rangle]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}[\|\nabla_{x}f(x_{\ast},Z^{(i)})-\mathbb{E}[\nabla_{x}f(x_{\ast},Z^{(i)})]\|^{2}]\leq\frac{B}{N},

where we have used that the
Z^{(1)},\dots,Z^{(N)}
are i.i.d. random variables. Together with inequality (
4.7
) we close the proof with

\mathbb{E}[\|X_{N}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}]\leq\frac{B}{\mu^{2}}\frac{1}{N}.

∎

The overall error of GD is then given by

\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[\|X_{k,N}^{\mathrm{GD}}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}]\leq c(\frac{1}{N}+\rho^{k})

for some constant
c>0
. Therefore, it is sufficient to choose
N\geq\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{-1})
and
k\geq\mathcal{O}(\log(\varepsilon^{-1})
, such that the computational cost of GD are given by

{\mathrm{cost}}_{\mathrm{GD}}(\varepsilon)=N\cdot k\simeq\varepsilon^{-1}\log(\varepsilon^{-1}),

whereas the computational cost of SGD are given by

{\mathrm{cost}}_{\mathrm{SGD}}(\varepsilon)=1\cdot k\simeq\varepsilon^{-1}.

Note that
\simeq
hides constants independent of
\varepsilon
.

4.3.6
Lower bound of SGD

In the following, we will observe that we do not expect to improve the derived upper bound on SGD in the strongly convex setting. Therefore, we consider the example of a simple quadratic function. The example to be considered has been studied in detail in
[
20
]
.

Example
\thethm

(SGD lower error bound)
.

Let
(Z_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of i.i.d. random variables in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with distribution
\mu_{Z}
and
\mathbb{E}[\|Z_{1}\|^{2}]<\infty
. We define

f(x,z)=\frac{1}{2}\|x-z\|^{2},\quad x,z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

and the corresponding expectation function

F(x)=\mathbb{E}[f(x,Z)],\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\quad Z\sim\mu_{Z}\,.

Firstly, observe that this expectation computes as

F(x)=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[\|x-Z\|^{2}]=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[\|x-\mathbb{E}[Z]\|^{2}]+\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[\|Z-\mathbb{E}[Z|\|^{2}]

such that we identify
x_{\ast}=\mathbb{E}[Z]\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
as the global minimum of
F
. In this formulation we are also able to compute exact derivatives of
F
and obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\|\nabla_{x}f(x,Z)-\nabla_{x}F(x)\|^{2}]=\mathbb{E}[\|(x-Z)-(x-\mathbb{E}[Z])\|^{2}]=\mathbb{E}[\|Z-\mathbb{E}[Z]\|^{2}]=:\sigma^{2}\,.

Next, consider the iteration generated by SGD with sequence of step sizes
\alpha_{k}=\frac{\tau}{k^{\nu}}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
for some
\nu>0
and
\tau>0
, which can be written as

X_{k+1}=X_{k}-\frac{\tau}{k^{\nu}}(X_{k}-Z_{k+1})=(1-\frac{\tau}{k^{\nu}})X_{k}+\frac{\tau}{k^{\nu}}Z_{k+1}\,.

In this example, the mean squared error can be computed explicitly,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\|X_{k+1}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\|X_{k+1}-\mathbb{E}[Z]\|^{2}]

\displaystyle=\left(1-\frac{\tau}{k^{\nu}}\right)^{2}\mathbb{E}[\|X_{k}-\mathbb{E}[Z]\|^{2}]+2\left(1-\frac{\tau}{k^{\nu}}\right)\frac{\tau}{k^{\nu}}\mathbb{E}[\langle X_{k}-\mathbb{E}[Z],Z_{k+1}-\mathbb{E}[Z]\rangle]

\displaystyle\quad+\left(\frac{\tau}{k^{\nu}}\right)^{2}\mathbb{E}[\|Z_{k+1}-\mathbb{E}[Z]\|^{2}]

\displaystyle=\left(1-\frac{\tau}{k^{\nu}}\right)^{2}\mathbb{E}[\|X_{k}-\mathbb{E}[Z]\|^{2}]+\left(\frac{\tau}{k^{\nu}}\right)^{2}\sigma^{2}\,.

It follows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\|X_{k}-\mathbb{E}[Z]\|^{2}]

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=0}^{k-1}\left(1-\frac{\tau}{j^{\nu}}\right)^{2}\mathbb{E}[\|X_{0}-\mathbb{E}[Z]\|^{2}]+\sigma^{2}\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\left(\frac{\tau}{k^{\nu}}\right)^{2}\prod_{i=j+1}^{k-1}\left(1-\frac{\tau}{i^{\nu}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\sigma^{2}\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\left(\frac{\tau}{k^{\nu}}\right)^{2}\prod_{i=j+1}^{k-1}\left(1-\frac{\tau}{i^{\nu}}\right)^{2}\,.

The last expression is bounded from below by
Ck^{-\nu}
for all sufficiently large
k\in\mathbb{N}
and some constant
C>0
; see
[
20
]
for details.
For the choice
\nu=1
, this lower bound matches the upper rate derived in Section
4.3.3
up to
constants.

4.4
Almost sure convergence rates

In
Section

4.2
we have proved almost sure convergence of SGD in the sense that the objective values converge almost surely to some limit and that

\liminf_{k\to\infty}\|\nabla_{x}F(X_{k})\|^{2}=0\quad\text{almost surely}.

The aim of the present section is to strengthen this statement by deriving almost sure convergence rates. The main idea is to combine the one-step descent estimate (
4.2
) with a rate version of the Robbins–Siegmund theorem. This approach follows the analysis of
[
30
,
44
]
, where almost sure convergence rates for SGD, stochastic heavy ball and stochastic Nesterov acceleration are derived under smoothness assumption. In this lecture, we restrict ourselves to the smooth non-convex and strongly convex case.

4.4.1
From supermartingales to almost sure convergence rates

We first collect two elementary rate lemmas for stochastic processes. The first one is useful in the strongly convex case, where the descent inequality contains a contraction term. The second one is used in the non-convex case, where only summability of the weighted gradient norm is available.

{lemma}

[Almost sure rates for contracting supermartingales]
Let
(Z_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of non-negative random variables adapted to
(\mathcal{F}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
. Let
\alpha_{k}=\frac{c_{\alpha}}{(k+1)^{\theta}}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
for some
\theta\in(\frac{1}{2},1)
. Suppose that there exist constants
c_{1},c_{2}>0
such that

\mathbb{E}[Z_{k+1}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]\leq(1-c_{1}\alpha_{k})Z_{k}+c_{2}\alpha_{k}^{2}

Then, for every
\eta\in(2-2\theta,1)
,

\lim_{k\to\infty}(k+1)^{1-\eta}Z_{k}=0\qquad\text{almost surely}.

Proof.

The proof follows the same idea as in the proof of Corollary
4.2
, but applied to a rescaled process. Define the adapted process
\widetilde{Z}_{k}:=(k+1)^{1-\eta}Z_{k}
.
Using the elementary estimate

(k+2)^{1-\eta}\leq(k+1)^{1-\eta}+(1-\eta)(k+1)^{-\eta},

we obtain, for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{Z}_{k+1}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]

\displaystyle=(k+2)^{1-\eta}\mathbb{E}[Z_{k+1}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]\leq(k+2)^{1-\eta}(1-c_{1}\alpha_{k})Z_{k}+c_{2}(k+2)^{1-\eta}\alpha_{k}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq(k+1)^{1-\eta}(1-\frac{c_{1}c_{\alpha}}{(k+1)^{\theta}})Z_{k}+c_{2}(k+1)^{1-\eta}\frac{c_{1}c_{\alpha}}{(k+1)^{2\theta}}

\displaystyle+(1-\eta)(k+1)^{-\eta}(1-\frac{c_{1}c_{\alpha}}{(k+1)^{\theta}})Z_{k}+(1-\eta)c_{2}(k+1)^{-\eta}\frac{c_{1}c_{\alpha}}{(k+1)^{2\theta}}

\displaystyle=(1+\frac{1-\eta}{k+1}-\frac{c_{1}c_{\alpha}}{(k+1)^{\theta}}-\frac{(1-\eta)c_{1}c_{\alpha}}{(k+1)^{\theta+1}})(k+1)^{1-\eta}Z_{k}

\displaystyle+\quad\frac{c_{1}c_{2}c_{\alpha}}{(k+1)^{2\theta+\eta-1}}+\frac{(1-\eta)c_{1}c_{2}c_{\alpha}}{(k+1)^{2\theta+\eta}}\,.

Since
\theta<1
, there exists
\bar{k}\geq 1
sufficiently large and
c_{3}>0
such that

\frac{1-\eta}{k+1}-\frac{c_{1}c_{\alpha}}{(k+1)^{\theta}}\leq-\frac{c_{3}}{(k+1)^{\theta}}

for all
k\geq\bar{k}
. Hence, for all
k\geq\bar{k}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{Z}_{k+1}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]\leq(1-\frac{c_{3}}{(k+1)^{\theta}})\tilde{Z}_{k}+\frac{c_{1}c_{2}c_{\alpha}}{(k+1)^{2\theta+\eta-1}}+\frac{(1-\eta)c_{1}c_{2}c_{\alpha}}{(k+1)^{2\theta+\eta}}\,.

Moreover, by assumption, we have

\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{c_{3}}{(k+1)^{\theta}}=\infty,\quad\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{c_{1}c_{2}c_{\alpha}}{(k+1)^{2\theta+\eta-1}}<\infty\quad\text{and}\quad\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(1-\eta)c_{1}c_{2}c_{\alpha}}{(k+1)^{2\theta+\eta}}<\infty\,.

Finally, Corollary
4.2
implies that
(\widetilde{Z}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
converges almost surely to zero.
∎

In the non-convex regime, we require the following adjusted statement.

{lemma}
[From summability to rates]
Let
(a_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be a decreasing sequence of strictly positive reals such that

\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a_{k}=\infty\quad\text{and}\quad\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{a_{k}}{\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}a_{i}}=\infty.

Suppose that
(Y_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is a sequence of non-negative random variables such that

\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a_{k}Y_{k}<\infty\qquad\text{almost surely}.

Then we have,

\lim_{k\to\infty}\ \min_{0\leq i\leq k}Y_{i}\,\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}a_{i}=0\qquad\text{almost surely}.

Proof.

We argue path-wise on the event
\{\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a_{k}Y_{k}<\infty\}
. Let

S_{k}:=\sum_{i=0}^{k}a_{i}.

Assume that the assertion is false. Then there exist
\delta>0
and infinitely many
k\in\mathbb{N}
(represented as a subsequence
(k_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
) such that

\min_{0\leq i\leq k}Y_{i}\,S_{k}\geq\delta.

Since
(S_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is increasing, this implies for the corresponding indices that

a_{i}Y_{i}\geq a_{i}\min_{0\leq j\leq k}Y_{i}\geq a_{i}\frac{\delta}{S_{k}}

for all
i=0,\dots,k_{n}
(and
n\in\mathbb{N}
). Summing over the indices gives,

\delta\lim_{n\to\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{k_{n}}\frac{a_{i}}{\sum_{j=0}^{k_{n}-1}a_{j}}\leq\lim_{n\to\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{k_{n}}a_{i}Y_{i}\,,

which yields a contradiction to the finiteness of
\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}a_{i}Y_{i}
.
∎

4.4.2
Non-convex and smooth objective functions

We first consider the smooth non-convex case. As already discussed, in the absence of convexity one can generally only expect convergence towards stationary points. Therefore, the natural quantity is again the gradient norm. This result is an almost sure counterpart to the classical convergence-rate statement in expectation from Theorem
4.3.1
. The essential ingredient is the summability property

\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{k}\|\nabla_{x}F(X_{k})\|^{2}<\infty\qquad\text{almost surely}.

{thm}

[Almost sure rate for the minimal gradient norm]
Suppose that the assumptions of Theorem
4.2
, and the additional assumptions that
\alpha_{k}\in(0,1/L]
and

\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{\alpha_{k}}{\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\alpha_{i}}=\infty

are in place. Then it holds that

\lim_{k\to\infty}\ \Big(\sum_{i=0}^{k}\alpha_{i}\Big)\min_{0\leq i\leq k}\|\nabla_{x}F(X_{i})\|^{2}=0\qquad\text{almost surely}.

In particular, for the choice

\alpha_{k}=\frac{\alpha}{(k+1)^{\frac{1}{2}+\eta}},\ \alpha\in(0,1/L],\ \eta\in(0,\tfrac{1}{2}),

we obtain

\lim_{k\to\infty}(k+1)^{\frac{1}{2}-\eta}\min_{0\leq i\leq k}\|\nabla_{x}F(X_{i})\|^{2}=0\qquad\text{almost surely}.

Proof.

Recall from (
4.2
), that for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[F(X_{k+1})-F^{\ast}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]

\displaystyle\leq(1+c\frac{L}{2}\alpha_{k}^{2})(F(X_{k})-F^{\ast})+c\frac{L}{2}\alpha_{k}^{2}-\alpha_{k}(1-\frac{L\alpha_{k}}{2})\|\nabla_{x}F(X_{k})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq(1+c\frac{L}{2}\alpha_{k}^{2})(F(X_{k})-F^{\ast})+c\frac{L}{2}\alpha_{k}^{2}-\frac{\alpha_{k}}{2}\|\nabla_{x}F(X_{k})\|^{2}\,.

By Theorem
4.2
,

\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{k}\|\nabla_{x}F(X_{k})\|^{2}<\infty\qquad\text{almost surely}.

Next, we apply Lemma
4.4.1
with

Y_{k}:=\|\nabla_{x}F(X_{k})\|^{2}\quad\text{and}\quad a_{k}:=\alpha_{k}\,,

which yields

\lim_{k\to\infty}\ \Big(\sum_{i=0}^{k}\alpha_{i}\Big)\min_{0\leq i\leq k}\|\nabla_{x}F(X_{i})\|^{2}=0\qquad\text{almost surely}.

For the polynomial step size

\alpha_{k}=\frac{\alpha}{(k+1)^{\frac{1}{2}+\eta}},

we can compute the growth of the accumulated step sizes explicitly. Set

S_{k}:=\sum_{i=0}^{k}\alpha_{i}=\alpha\sum_{i=0}^{k}\frac{1}{(i+1)^{\frac{1}{2}+\eta}}=\alpha\sum_{j=1}^{k+1}\frac{1}{j^{\frac{1}{2}+\eta}}

and lower bound this sum via an integral similar as in the proof of Corollary
4.3.1
.
Since the function
x\mapsto x^{-\frac{1}{2}-\eta}
is positive and decreasing on
[1,\infty)
, one has:

\sum_{j=1}^{k+1}\frac{1}{j^{\frac{1}{2}+\eta}}\geq\int_{1}^{k+2}x^{-\frac{1}{2}-\eta}\,{\mathrm{d}}x\,.

Due to
\eta\in(0,\tfrac{1}{2})
, we have
\frac{1}{2}-\eta>0
, and hence

S_{k}\geq\alpha\,\int_{1}^{k+2}x^{-\frac{1}{2}-\eta}\,{\mathrm{d}}x=\frac{\alpha}{\frac{1}{2}-\eta}\left((k+2)^{\frac{1}{2}-\eta}-1\right)\,.

This implies that there exists a constant
B>0
and an index
k_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that

S_{k}=\sum_{i=0}^{k}\alpha_{i}\geq Bk^{\frac{1}{2}-\eta}\qquad\text{for all }k\geq k_{0}\,.

In summary, this verifies that

\lim_{k\to\infty}\ k^{\frac{1}{2}-\eta}\min_{0\leq i\leq k}\|\nabla_{x}F(X_{i})\|^{2}\leq\frac{1}{B}\lim_{k\to\infty}S_{k}\min_{0\leq i\leq k}\|\nabla_{x}F(X_{i})\|^{2}=0\qquad\text{almost surely}\,.

∎

4.4.3
Strongly convex and smooth objective functions

We now turn to the strongly convex case. Recall that if
F
is
\mu
-strongly convex, then there exists a unique global minimizer
x_{\ast}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and by Proposition
2.2.6
it satisfies the PL condition

\|\nabla_{x}F(x)\|^{2}\geq 2\mu(F(x)-F(x_{\ast})).

In contrast to the proof of Theorem
4.3.3
and Theorem
4.3.4
, we work with the recursion under conditional expectation rather than full expectation. Note that the following result is directly based on the proof of Theorem
4.3.4
, and a similar statement can be derived in the setting of Theorem
4.3.3
.

{thm}
[Almost sure rate under strong convexity]
Suppose that the assumptions of Theorem
4.3.4
are in place, and the additional requirement that

\alpha_{k}=\frac{\alpha}{(k+1)^{1-\theta}}\

for some
\alpha\in(0,1/L]
and
\theta\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
holds.

Then, for every
\eta\in(2\theta,1)
, it holds that

\lim_{k\to\infty}\ k^{1-\eta}(F(X_{k})-F(x_{\ast}))=0\qquad\text{almost surely}.

Proof.

We define

Y_{k}:=F(X_{k})-F(x_{\ast}).

Similarly to the proof of Theorem
4.3.4
, combining (
4.2
) and the PL condition, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[Y_{k+1}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]

\displaystyle\leq Y_{k}-\frac{\alpha_{k}}{2}\|\nabla_{x}F(X_{k})\|^{2}+\frac{LC}{2}\alpha_{k}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq Y_{k}-\mu\alpha_{k}Y_{k}+\frac{LC}{2}\alpha_{k}^{2}\,.

Note that we have used
\alpha_{k}\leq 1/L
. Therefore, Lemma
4.4.1
yields

\lim_{k\to\infty}\ k^{1-\eta}Y_{k}=0\qquad\text{almost surely}

for every
\eta\in(2\theta,1)
. This proves the first assertion.
∎

The parameter
\theta\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
can be chosen arbitrarily small, and then
\eta>2\theta
can also be chosen arbitrarily small. Hence, the rate in Theorem
4.4.3
can get arbitrarily close to
1/k
, the derived rate in Theorem
4.3.4
for the convergence in expectation.

4.5
Variance reduction

In the derived convergence results for SGD we have always assumed that
\nabla_{x}f(x,Z)
is an unbiased estimator of
\nabla_{x}F(x)
with uniformly bounded variance

\mathbb{E}[\|\nabla_{x}f(x,Z)-\nabla_{x}F(x)\|^{2}]\leq{\mathrm{var}}\,.

This upper bound
{\mathrm{var}}>0
occurs in all derived error bounds in a similar way:

Assumption

error bound

convex

\frac{C_{1}}{\sqrt{k}}+{\mathrm{var}}\cdot\frac{\log(k)}{\sqrt{k}}

strong convex

(1-\alpha_{k}\mu)e_{k}+{\mathrm{var}}\cdot\alpha_{k}^{2}

PL-condition

(1-\alpha_{k}r)e_{k}+{\mathrm{var}}\cdot\alpha_{k}^{2}

In order to push the total error towards zero, we had to choose
\alpha_{k}\to 0
. In the specific cases of strong convexity or under PL-condition, we lose the behavior of linear convergence which we obtained for the exact (deterministic) gradient descent scheme. In the deterministic setting, we were able to choose
\alpha_{k}=\bar{\alpha}>0
such that

e_{k+1}\leq\rho(\bar{\alpha})e_{k},\quad\rho(\bar{\alpha})\in(0,1)\,

By controlling the variance error term through
\alpha_{k}\to 0
, we obtain an error bound for SGD of the form

e_{k+1}\leq\rho_{k}e_{k}+{\mathrm{var}}_{k},

where
\rho_{k}\to 1
for
k\to\infty
. This behavior makes the analysis challenging and in particular, we have seen that SGD does not converge linearly.

In the following section, we consider different types of variance reduction methods, which control the variance error term in a different way. We are no longer forced to consider
\alpha_{k}\to 0
and will choose a fixed step size
\alpha_{k}=\bar{\alpha}>0
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

4.5.1
Dynamic Sampling

Our first method to be considered is called
dynamic sampling
, where we control the variance error term through a dynamical batch-sampling strategy. The unbiased estimator of the descent direction
\nabla_{x}F(X_{k})
is estimated through a batch of samples
(Z_{k}^{(m)})_{k\in\mathbb{N},\ m=1,\dots,B_{k-1}}
, where the random variables are assumed to be independent in
k
and in
m
with an identical distribution
\mu_{Z}
.

1:

Input:

•

loss function
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{p}\to\mathbb{R}

•

initial random variable
X_{0}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{d}

•

sequence of step sizes
(\alpha_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
,
\alpha_{k}>0
(deterministic or
\mathcal{F}
-adapted)

•

sequence of batch sizes
(B_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}

•

sequence of i.i.d. random variables
(Z_{k}^{(m)})_{k\in\mathbb{N},\ m=1,\dots,B_{k-1}}
with
Z_{1}^{(1)}\sim\mu_{Z}
.

2:

set
k=0

3:

while
”convergence/stopping criterion not met”
do

4:

approximate the gradient
\nabla_{x}F(X_{k})
through

G_{k}=\frac{1}{B_{k}}\sum_{m=1}^{B_{k}}\nabla_{x}f(X_{k},Z_{k+1}^{(m)})

5:

set
X_{k+1}=X_{k}-\alpha_{k}G_{k}
,
k\mapsto k+1

6:

end

while

Algorithm 8

SGD with dynamic sampling

In the following, we will analyze SGD with dynamical batch-sampling. The focus will be placed on the strongly convex setting and the method will be compared to a fixed batch size
\bar{B}>0
across all iterations. For both schemes, we will consider a fixed step size
\bar{\alpha}>0
.

Firstly, we discuss how the assumed uniform upper bound on the variance is effected through the incorporation of batch-sampling.

{lemma}

Let the assumptions of Lemma
4.1.2
be satisfied and assume that

\mathbb{E}[\|\nabla_{x}f(x,Z)-\nabla_{x}F(x)\|^{2}]\leq c

for some
c>0
and all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Moreover, let
Z^{(1)},\dots,Z^{(B)}
be i.i.d. random variables with distribution
\mu_{Z}
. Then for
G:=\frac{1}{B}\sum_{m=1}^{B}\nabla_{x}f(x,Z^{(m)})
it holds true that

\mathbb{E}[\|G-\nabla_{x}F(x)\|^{2}\leq\frac{c}{B}

for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Proof.

By
\mathbb{E}[\nabla_{x}f(x,Z^{(1)})]=\nabla_{x}F(x)
and the independence of
Z^{(1)},\dots,Z^{(m)}
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\|G-\nabla_{x}F(x)\|^{2}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{B^{2}}\sum_{m,n=1}^{B}\mathbb{E}[\langle\nabla_{x}f(x,Z^{(m)})-\nabla_{x}F(x),\nabla_{x}f(x,Z^{(n)})-\nabla_{x}F(x)\rangle]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{B^{2}}\sum_{m=1}^{B}\mathbb{E}[\|\nabla_{x}f(x,Z^{(m)})-\nabla_{x}F(x)\|^{2}]\leq\frac{c}{B}\,.

∎

With the previous observations we are now able to extend Theorem
4.3.3
to dynamical batch-sampling.

{thm}
[SGD with dynamic sampling]
Let
F:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be
\mu
-strongly convex and
L
-smooth. We assume that the assumptions of Lemma
4.1.2
are satisfied and that there exists
c>0
such that

\mathbb{E}[\|\nabla_{x}f(x,Z)-\mathbb{E}[\nabla_{x}f(x,Z)]\|^{2}]\leq c

for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Let
X_{0}
be a random variable such that
\mathbb{E}[|F(X_{0})|+\|X_{0}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}]<\infty
, where
x_{\ast}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is the unique global minimum of
F
. Moreover, let
(X_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be generated by Algorithm
8
with sequence of batch sizes
(B_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
,
B_{k}\geq 1
and deterministic, decreasing sequence of step sizes
(\alpha_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
\alpha_{k}\in(0,\frac{1}{L}]
. Then for the error
e_{k}:=\mathbb{E}[\|X_{k}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}]
it holds true that

e_{k+1}\leq(1-\alpha_{k}\mu)e_{k}+\frac{c\alpha_{k}^{2}}{B_{k}}

for all
k\geq 0
. Furthermore, for a fixed step size
\alpha_{k}=\bar{\alpha}=\frac{\tau}{\mu}
with
\tau\leq\frac{1}{\kappa}=\frac{\mu}{L}
, it holds true that

e_{k+1}\leq\rho e_{k}+\frac{c\bar{\alpha}^{2}}{B_{k}}

(4.8)

where
\rho=(1-\tau)\in(0,1)
.

Proof.

The proof follows by Theorem
4.3.3
combined with Lemma
4.5.1
.
∎

In the following, we will keep the step size
\bar{\alpha}=\frac{\tau}{\mu}\leq\frac{1}{L}
fixed and aim to derive an optimal sequence of batch sizes. Firstly, we need to formulate what we mean by an
optimal
batch size. Therefore, we will assume that the computation of each iteration of SGD occurs with cost which are determined through the generation of the samples
(Z_{k}^{(m)})_{m=1,\dots,B_{k-1}}
. We will assume that these cost are normalized.

{ass}

The generation of the state
X_{k}
by Algorithm
8
with sequence of batch sizes
(B_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
occurs with computational cost

{\mathrm{cost}}(X_{k})=\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}B_{j}\,.

We want to choose the batch sizes such that we minimize the computational cost under the constraint that the final error bound is below a specified tolerance level
\varepsilon>0
, i.e. we want to solve the constrained minimization problem

\min_{B_{0},\dots,B_{K-1}}\ \sum_{j=0}^{K-1}B_{j}\quad\text{s.t.}\ e_{K}\leq\varepsilon\,.

We refer the interested reader to
[
47
]
, where this approach has been considered in a more general framework. The total error after iteration
K\geq 1
can be upper bounded by iterating the error bound (
4.8
):

e_{K}\leq\rho^{K}e_{0}+c\bar{\alpha}^{2}\sum_{j=0}^{K-1}\rho^{K-1-j}\frac{1}{B_{j}}\,.

For simplicity, we assume that
K\geq\lceil\log(\rho)^{-1}\log(\frac{\varepsilon}{2}e_{0}^{-1})\rceil
such that

e_{K}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}.

For this given
K
we want to determine
B_{0},\dots,B_{K-1}
under the constrain

c\bar{\alpha}^{2}\sum_{j=0}^{K-1}\rho^{K-1-j}\frac{1}{B_{j}}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\,.

We start with the following auxiliary result:

{lemma}

Let
\varepsilon>0
,
\gamma>0
and
a_{j}>0
,
j\in\{0,\dots,K-1\}
. Then the choice

B_{j}=C(\varepsilon,K)\cdot a_{j}^{\frac{1}{1+\gamma}},\quad C(\varepsilon,K)=\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{\gamma}}\left(\sum_{s=0}^{K-1}a_{s}^{\frac{1}{1+\gamma}}\right)^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}

solves the constrained optimization problem

\min_{B_{0},\dots,B_{K-1}}\ \sum_{j=0}^{K-1}B_{j},\quad\text{s.t.}\ \sum_{j=0}^{K-1}a_{j}B_{j}^{-\gamma}\leq\varepsilon\,.

Proof.

We only derive a stationary point of the considered constrained optimization problem.
The Lagrange function is given by

\mathcal{L}(B_{0},\dots,B_{K-1},\lambda)=\sum_{j=0}^{K-1}B_{j}+\lambda\left(\sum_{j=0}^{K-1}a_{j}B_{j}^{-\gamma}-\varepsilon\right)

and the corresponding optimality conditions are

\displaystyle({\mathrm{I}})

\displaystyle\quad 1-\lambda-\lambda\gamma a_{j}B_{j}^{-(1+\gamma)}=0,\ j=0,\dots,K-1,

\displaystyle({\mathrm{II}})

\displaystyle\quad\sum_{j=0}^{K-1}a_{j}B_{j}^{-\gamma}-\varepsilon=0\,.

We solve
({\mathrm{I}})
to derive

B_{j}=(\lambda\gamma)^{\frac{1}{1+\gamma}}a_{j}^{\frac{1}{1+\gamma}},

which together with
({\mathrm{II}})
gives

(\lambda\gamma)^{-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma}}\sum_{j=0}^{K-1}a_{j}\cdot a_{j}^{-\frac{\gamma}{\gamma+1}}=(\lambda\gamma)^{-\frac{\gamma}{1+\gamma}}\sum_{j=0}^{K-1}a_{j}^{\frac{1}{\gamma+1}}=\varepsilon

and therefore,

\lambda\gamma=\varepsilon^{-\frac{1+\gamma}{\gamma}}\left(\sum_{j=0}^{K-1}a_{j}^{\frac{1}{1+\gamma}}\right)^{\frac{1+\gamma}{\gamma}}\,.

This results in

B_{j}=C(\varepsilon,K)\cdot a_{j}^{\frac{1}{1+\gamma}},\quad C(\varepsilon,K)=\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{\gamma}}\left(\sum_{s=0}^{K-1}a_{s}^{\frac{1}{1+\gamma}}\right)^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}\,.

∎

We are now ready to choose the optimal batch size for Algorithm
8
under strong convexity assumption:

\min_{B_{0},\dots,B_{K-1}}\ \sum_{j=0}^{K-1}B_{j},\quad\text{s.t.}\ \sum_{j=0}^{K-1}c\bar{\alpha}^{2}\rho^{K-1-j}B_{j}^{-1}\leq\varepsilon/2\,,

which by Lemma
4.5.1
leads to

C(\varepsilon,K)=2\varepsilon^{-1}\sqrt{c}\bar{\alpha}\sum_{j=0}^{K-1}\rho^{\frac{K-1-j}{2}}=2\varepsilon^{-1}\sqrt{c}\bar{\alpha}\sum_{j=0}^{K-1}\rho^{\frac{j}{2}}=2\varepsilon^{-1}\sqrt{c}\bar{\alpha}\left(\frac{1-\rho^{\frac{K}{2}}}{1-\rho^{\frac{1}{2}}}\right)

and therefore, to an optimal dynamical batch size

B_{j}=2\varepsilon^{-1}c\bar{\alpha}^{2}\left(\frac{1-\rho^{\frac{K}{2}}}{1-\rho^{\frac{1}{2}}}\right)\rho^{\frac{K-1-j}{2}}\,.

(4.9)

The corresponding computational cost are given by

\sum_{j=0}^{K-1}B_{j}=2\varepsilon^{-1}c\bar{\alpha}^{2}\left(\frac{1-\rho^{\frac{K}{2}}}{1-\rho^{\frac{1}{2}}}\right)\sum_{j=0}^{K-1}\rho^{\frac{K-1-j}{2}}=2\varepsilon^{-1}c\bar{\alpha}^{2}\left(\frac{1-\rho^{\frac{K}{2}}}{1-\rho^{\frac{1}{2}}}\right)^{2}\simeq\varepsilon^{-1},

where
\left(\frac{1-\rho^{\frac{K}{2}}}{1-\rho^{\frac{1}{2}}}\right)^{2}\in\left(1,\left(\frac{1}{1-\sqrt{\rho}}\right)^{2}\right)
, independent of
K
. We compare the derived dynamical batch-sampling strategy to a fixed batch size
\bar{B}\geq 1
for all
k=0,\dots,K-1
. This fixed batch size has again to be chosen such that
e_{K}\leq\varepsilon
. For simplicity, let again
K\geq\lceil\log(\rho^{-1})\log(\frac{\varepsilon}{2}e_{0}^{-1})\rceil
such that
\rho^{K}e_{0}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}
and therefore,

e_{K}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}+c\bar{\alpha}^{2}\frac{1}{\bar{B}}\sum_{j=0}^{K-1}\rho^{K-1-j}=\frac{\varepsilon}{2}+c\bar{\alpha}^{2}\frac{1-\rho^{K}}{1-\rho}\frac{1}{\bar{B}},

where
\frac{1-\rho^{K}}{1-\rho}\leq\frac{1}{1-\rho}
. The fixed batch size
\bar{B}
needs to be chosen such that

\bar{B}\geq 2\varepsilon^{-1}c\bar{\alpha}^{2}(1-\rho)^{-1}\simeq\varepsilon^{-1}

(4.10)

and the corresponding computational cost are given by

\sum_{j=0}^{K-1}B_{j}=K\cdot\bar{B}\simeq|\log(\varepsilon^{-1})|\varepsilon^{-1}\,.

We summarize the derived batch-sampling strategies in the following theorem.

{thm}

Suppose that the conditions of Theorem
4.5.1
are satisfied and define
e_{0}=\mathbb{E}[\|X_{0}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}]
. Let
\varepsilon>0
and
K\geq\lceil\log(\rho^{-1})\log(\frac{\varepsilon}{2}e_{0}^{-1})\rceil
. Let
(X_{k}^{\mathrm{DS}})_{k=0,\dots,K}
be generated by Algorithm
8
with the dynamical batch sizes

(B_{k})_{k=0,\dots,K-1}
defined in (
4.9
). Moreover, let

(X_{k}^{\mathrm{FB}})_{k=0,\dots,K}
be generated by Algorithm
8
with the fixed batch size
B_{k}=\bar{B}
from (
4.10
).
Then

\displaystyle e_{K}^{\mathrm{DS}}:=\mathbb{E}[\|X_{K}^{\mathrm{DS}}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon\quad\text{and}\quad e_{K}^{\mathrm{FB}}:=\mathbb{E}[\|X_{K}^{\mathrm{FB}}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}]\leq\varepsilon\,,

while the computational cost is given by

\displaystyle{\mathrm{cost}}^{\mathrm{DS}}:={\mathrm{cost}}(X_{K}^{\mathrm{DS}})=\sum_{j=0}^{K-1}B_{j}

\displaystyle\simeq\varepsilon^{-1},

\displaystyle{\mathrm{cost}}^{\mathrm{FB}}:={\mathrm{cost}}(X_{K}^{\mathrm{FB}})=K\cdot\bar{B}

\displaystyle\simeq|\log(\varepsilon^{-1})|\varepsilon^{-1}\,.

4.5.2
Stochastic average gradient method (SAG)

In the following three subsections, we consider variance reduction methods for finite-sum optimization problems. We fix the realization of the data set
\{z^{(1)},\dots,z^{(N)}\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{p}
and focus on the empirical objective

F_{N}(x)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}f(x,z^{(i)})=:\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}f_{i}(x),\qquad x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

where
f_{i}(x):=f(x,z^{(i)})
. In this section, we do not study the statistical error caused by replacing the expected risk by the empirical risk.

If
F_{N}
is
\mu
-strongly convex and
L
-smooth, the deterministic gradient descent scheme applied to
F_{N}
converges linearly to its unique minimizer. However, each iteration requires the evaluation of the full gradient

\nabla F_{N}(x_{k})=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\nabla f_{i}(x_{k}),

and therefore one pass over the entire data set. In contrast, finite-sum SGD evaluates only one component gradient
\nabla f_{i_{k}}(x_{k})
per iteration. This reduces the cost of a single iteration, but the persistent variance of the stochastic
gradient estimator prevents the same linear convergence behavior with a fixed step size. Variance reduction methods are designed to combine the advantages of both approaches: cheap stochastic updates and linear convergence under strong convexity.
In each iteration, the goal is to evaluate only one new gradient across the family of functions
\{f_{i}\}_{i=1,\dots,N}
such as it is the case in Algorithm
7
. Note that all of these algorithms essentially assume that the objective function is in the form of a finite sum
F_{N}
.

In the following it is clear out of context, that the gradient
\nabla_{x}f_{i}(\cdot)
is computed wrt.
x
, such that from now on we will omit the dependence on
x
and simply write
\nabla f_{i}(\cdot)
.

Motivation:

Suppose that we want to estimate an unknown parameter
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
and
G
be an unbiased estimator of
\theta
, i.e.
\mathbb{E}[G]=\theta
. Moreover, let
\xi
be a random variable with mean close to zero,
\mathbb{E}[\xi]\approx 0
, such that the modified random variable
G_{\xi}:=G-\xi
is
nearly
unbiased, i.e.
\mathbb{E}[G_{\xi}]=\mathbb{E}[G]-\mathbb{E}[\xi]\approx\theta
. (In case
\mathbb{E}[\xi]=0
,
G_{\xi}
even remains unbiased). The modification becomes interesting when considering the resulting variance:

\mathbb{V}(G_{\xi})=\mathbb{V}(G-\xi)=\mathbb{V}(G)+\mathbb{V}(\xi)-2{\mathrm{Cov}}(G,\xi)\,.

So in case we find a high (positive) correlation between
G
and
\xi
, we hope for a significant reduction of the variance without introducing a large bias. This concept can be viewed as motivation for the following three algorithms to be considered.

We consider the first algorithm which forms the basis for introducing variance reduction in SGD. In
[
43
]
the authors propose the
stochastic average gradient
(SAG) method, which achieves linear convergence for strongly convex objective functions while having same complexity characteristics as SGD. The idea is to reuse the gradient information obtained from the past.

1:

Input:

•

objective function
F_{N}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
,
F_{N}(x)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}f_{i}(x)

•

initial random variable
X_{0}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{d}

•

sequence of step sizes
(\alpha_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
,
\alpha_{k}>0
(deterministic or
\mathcal{F}
-adapted)

2:

set
k=0
, initialize
G_{0}^{(i)}=0
,
i=1,\dots,N

3:

compute
\bar{G}_{0}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}G_{0}^{(i)}

4:

while
”convergence/stopping criterion not met”
do

5:

generate independently
\mathfrak{i}_{k+1}\sim\mathcal{U}(\{1,\dots,N\})

6:

set
G_{k}^{(i)}=\begin{cases}\nabla f_{i}(X_{k}),&i=\mathfrak{i}_{k+1}\\
G_{k-1}^{(i)},&i\neq\mathfrak{i}_{k+1}\end{cases}

7:

approximate the gradient
\nabla F_{N}(X_{k})
through

\bar{G}_{k}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}G_{k}^{(i)}=\bar{G}_{k-1}-\frac{1}{N}G_{k-1}^{(\mathfrak{i}_{k+1})}+\frac{1}{N}\nabla f_{\mathfrak{i}_{k+1}}(X_{k})

8:

set
X_{k+1}=X_{k}-\alpha_{k}\bar{G}_{k}
,
k\mapsto k+1

9:

end

while

Algorithm 9

Stochastic average gradient method (SAG)

The algorithm stores all gradient approximations across the entire data set
i=1,\dots,N
, and in each iteration it updates the approximation of the gradient
f_{\mathfrak{i}}
for only one randomly picked index
\mathfrak{i}
. Similarly to SGD in the form of Algorithm
7
, only one new gradient needs to be evaluated per iteration. However, one needs to have capacity for storing the gradient approximation for each index
i=1,\dots,N
, which can be seen as the main disadvantage of SAG. In contrast to the basic SGD estimator, the SAG estimator is generally biased. As mentioned above, the algorithm achieves linear convergence toward the global optimum of
F_{N}
as shown in
[
43
]
.

{thm}

[Theorem 1 in
[
43
]
]
Let
F_{N},\ f_{i}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
,
i=1,\dots,N
, be
\mu
-strongly convex and
L
-smooth. Moreover, let
(X_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be generated by Algorithm
9
with fixed step size
\alpha_{k}=\bar{\alpha}=\frac{1}{16L}
. Then it holds true that

\frac{\mu}{2}\mathbb{E}[\|X_{k}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}]\leq\mathbb{E}[F_{N}(X_{k})-F_{N}(x_{\ast})]\leq\left(1-\min\left\{\frac{\mu}{16L},\frac{1}{8N}\right\}\right)^{k}C_{0},

where
C_{0}=\mathbb{E}[F_{N}(X_{0})-F_{N}(x_{\ast})]+\frac{4L}{N}\mathbb{E}[\|X_{0}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}]+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{16L}
with
\sigma^{2}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\|\nabla f_{i}(x_{\ast})\|^{2}
.

Remark
\thethm
.

Ignoring the cost of storing
\{G_{k}^{(i)}\}_{i=1,\dots,N}
we can run
N
iterations of Algorithm
9
to achieve a similar complexity as the deterministic full gradient descent scheme. To compare both SAG and deterministic GD we can view SAG as linearly converging scheme with rate

\rho^{\mathrm{SAG}}=\left(1-\min\left\{\frac{\mu}{16L},\frac{1}{8N}\right\}\right)^{N}\,.

For small
N
(
N\leq\frac{2L}{\mu}
) the rate is dominated by

\rho^{\mathrm{SAG}}=\left(1-\frac{\mu}{16L}\right)^{N}

which corresponds to
N
steps of GD with step size
\bar{\alpha}=\frac{1}{16L}
. In comparison, for large
N
(
N\geq\frac{2L}{\mu}=2\kappa
) the rate is dominated by

\rho^{\mathrm{SAG}}=\left(1-\frac{1}{8N}\right)^{N}\leq\exp(-\frac{1}{8})\,,

such that the rate can be uniformly controlled in
N
.

4.5.3
SAGA

The convergence analysis of SAG is challenging due to the biased estimation of the gradients
\nabla F_{N}(X_{k})
in each iteration:

\mathbb{E}[\bar{G}_{k}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]=\underbrace{\mathbb{E}[\bar{G}_{k-1}-\frac{1}{N}G_{k-1}^{(\mathfrak{i}_{k+1})}]}_{\neq 0}+\frac{1}{N}\nabla F_{N}(X_{k})=(1-\frac{1}{N})\bar{G}_{k-1}+\frac{1}{N}\nabla F_{N}(X_{k})\,.

This problem can be solved by replacing the approximation of
\nabla F_{N}(X_{k})
through an unbiased estimator of form

\bar{G}_{k}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}G_{k-1}^{(i)}-G_{k-1}^{(\mathfrak{i}_{k+1})}+\nabla f_{\mathfrak{i}_{k+1}}(X_{k})\,.

This estimator is unbiased since

\mathbb{E}[\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}G_{k-1}^{(i)}-G_{k-1}^{(\mathfrak{i}_{k+1})}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]=0\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{E}[\nabla f_{\mathfrak{i}_{k+1}}(X_{k})\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]=\nabla F_{N}(X_{k})\,.

Note that
\bar{G}_{k}
does not correspond to the mean over all stored gradients
\bar{G}_{k}\neq\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}G_{k}^{(i)}
anymore.

This observation led to a modified algorithm called
SAGA
which has been introduced in
[
7
]
.

1:

Input:

•

objective function
F_{N}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
,
F_{N}(x)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}f_{i}(x)

•

initial random variable
X_{0}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{d}

•

sequence of step sizes
(\alpha_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
,
\alpha_{k}>0
(deterministic or
\mathcal{F}
-adapted)

2:

set
k=0
, initialize
G_{0}^{(i)}=0
,
i=1,\dots,N

3:

compute
\bar{G}_{0}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}G_{0}^{(i)}

4:

while
”convergence/stopping criterion not met”
do

5:

generate independently
\mathfrak{i}_{k+1}\sim\mathcal{U}(\{1,\dots,N\})

6:

set
G_{k}^{(i)}=\begin{cases}\nabla f_{i}(X_{k}),&i=\mathfrak{i}_{k+1}\\
G_{k-1}^{(i)},&i\neq\mathfrak{i}_{k+1}\end{cases}

7:

approximate the gradient
\nabla F_{N}(X_{k})
through

\bar{G}_{k}=\bar{G}_{k-1}-G_{k-1}^{(\mathfrak{i}_{k+1})}+\nabla f_{\mathfrak{i}_{k+1}}(X_{k})

8:

set
X_{k+1}=X_{k}-\alpha_{k}\bar{G}_{k}
,
k\mapsto k+1

9:

end

while

Algorithm 10

SAGA

We follow the proof of linear convergence under strong convexity for Algorithm
10
presented in
[
7
]
. Let us assume that
F_{N}
,
f_{i}
,
i=1,\dots,N
are
\mu
-strongly convex and
L
-smooth. For
X_{0}(\omega)=x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we define point-wise

\phi_{0}^{(i)}(\omega)=x_{0},\quad\phi_{k+1}^{(i)}(\omega)=\begin{cases}\phi_{k}^{(i)}(\omega),&i\neq\mathfrak{i}_{k+1}(\omega)\\
X_{k}(\omega),&i=\mathfrak{i}_{k+1}(\omega)\end{cases}

and consider the error function of form

\displaystyle E_{k}=\underbrace{c\|X_{k}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}}_{=:E_{k}^{(2)}}+\underbrace{\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(f_{i}(\phi_{k}^{(i)})-f_{i}(x_{\ast})-\langle\nabla f_{i}(x_{\ast}),\phi_{k}^{(i)}-x_{\ast}\rangle\right)}_{=:E_{k}^{(1)}}\geq c\|X_{k}-x_{\ast}\|^{2},

where
x_{\ast}=\arg\min_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\ F_{N}(x)
. Note that
E_{k}^{(1)}\geq 0
by convexity of
f_{i}
. We observe that

\|X_{k+1}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}=\|X_{k}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}-2\bar{\alpha}\langle X_{k}-x_{\ast},\bar{G}_{k}\rangle+\bar{\alpha}^{2}\|\bar{G}_{k}\|^{2}

and with
\mathcal{F}_{k}:=\sigma(X_{0},\mathfrak{i}_{m},m\leq k)
and
\mathbb{E}[\bar{G}_{k}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]=\nabla F_{N}(X_{k})
we have that

\mathbb{E}[\|X_{k+1}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]=\|X_{k}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}-2\bar{\alpha}\langle X_{k}-x_{\ast},\nabla F_{N}(X_{k})\rangle+\bar{\alpha}^{2}\mathbb{E}[\|\bar{G}_{k}\|^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]\,.

In order to obtain an improved convergence result compared to SGD, we need to control
\mathbb{E}[\|\bar{G}_{k}\|^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]
sufficiently well.

{lemma}

Let
F_{N},\ f_{i}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
,
i=1,\dots,N
, be
\mu
-strongly convex and
L
-smooth. Then for any
\beta>0
it holds true that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\|\bar{G}_{k}\|^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]

\displaystyle\leq(1+\beta^{-1})\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\|\nabla f_{i}(\phi_{k}^{(i)})-\nabla f_{i}(x_{\ast})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+(1+\beta)\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\|\nabla f_{i}(X_{k})-\nabla f_{i}(x_{\ast})\|^{2}-\beta\|\nabla F_{N}(X_{k})\|^{2}\,,

where
x_{\ast}=\arg\min_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\ F_{N}(x)
.

Proof.

We will apply multiple times the following equality

\mathbb{E}[\|Q-\mathbb{E}[Q]\|^{2}]=\mathbb{E}[\|Q\|^{2}]-\|\mathbb{E}[Q]\|^{2}

(4.11)

for any random vector
Q
with
\mathbb{E}[\|Q\|^{2}]<\infty
. By construction of
\bar{G}_{k}
we can write

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\|\bar{G}_{k}\|^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\nabla f_{i}(\phi_{k}^{(i)})-\nabla f_{\mathfrak{i}_{k+1}}(\phi_{k}^{(\mathfrak{i}_{k+1})})+\nabla f_{\mathfrak{i}_{k+1}}(X_{k})\|^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]

\displaystyle=:\mathbb{E}[\|Q\|^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]=\mathbb{E}[\|Q-\mathbb{E}[Q\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]\|^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]+\|\mathbb{E}[Q\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\nabla f_{i}(\phi_{k}^{(i)})-\nabla f_{\mathfrak{i}_{k+1}}(\phi_{k}^{(\mathfrak{i}_{k+1})})+\nabla f_{\mathfrak{i}_{k+1}}(X_{k})-\nabla F_{N}(X_{k})\|^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]

\displaystyle\quad+\|\nabla F_{N}(X_{k})\|^{2}

Let us consider the first term

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\nabla f_{i}(\phi_{k}^{(i)})-\nabla f_{\mathfrak{i}_{k+1}}(\phi_{k}^{(\mathfrak{i}_{k+1})})+\nabla f_{\mathfrak{i}_{k+1}}(X_{k})-\nabla F_{N}(X_{k})\|^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\|\left\{\nabla f_{\mathfrak{i}_{k+1}}(\phi_{k}^{(\mathfrak{i}_{k+1})})-\nabla f_{\mathfrak{i}_{k+1}}(x_{\ast})-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\nabla f_{i}(\phi_{k}^{(i)})\right\}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad-\left\{\nabla f_{\mathfrak{i}_{k+1}}(X_{k})-\nabla f_{\mathfrak{i}_{k+1}}(x_{\ast})-\nabla F_{N}(X_{k})\right\}\|^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]

\displaystyle\leq(1+\beta^{-1})\mathbb{E}[\|\nabla f_{\mathfrak{i}_{k+1}}(\phi_{k}^{(\mathfrak{i}_{k+1})})-\nabla f_{\mathfrak{i}_{k+1}}(x_{\ast})-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\nabla f_{i}(\phi_{k}^{(i)})\|^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]

\displaystyle\quad+(1+\beta)\mathbb{E}[\|\nabla f_{\mathfrak{i}_{k+1}}(X_{k})-\nabla f_{\mathfrak{i}_{k+1}}(x_{\ast})-\nabla F_{N}(X_{k})\|^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]\,,

where we have used that
\|x+y\|^{2}\leq(1+\beta^{-1})\|x\|^{2}+(1+\beta)\|y\|^{2}
for any
\beta>0
. We define
Q_{1}=\nabla f_{\mathfrak{i}_{k+1}}(\phi_{k}^{(\mathfrak{i}_{k+1})})-\nabla f_{\mathfrak{i}_{k+1}}(x_{\ast})
with

\mathbb{E}[Q_{1}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\nabla f_{i}(\phi_{k}^{(i)})-\nabla F_{N}(x_{\ast})=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\nabla f_{i}(\phi_{k}^{(i)})\,,

and similarly

Q_{2}=\nabla f_{\mathfrak{i}_{k+1}}(X_{k})-\nabla f_{\mathfrak{i}_{k+1}}(x_{\ast})\quad\text{with}\quad\mathbb{E}[Q_{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]=\nabla F_{N}(X_{k})\,.

Finally, we obtain

\displaystyle(1+\beta^{-1})\mathbb{E}[\|\nabla f_{\mathfrak{i}_{k+1}}(\phi_{k}^{(\mathfrak{i}_{k+1})})-\nabla f_{\mathfrak{i}_{k+1}}(x_{\ast})-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\nabla f_{i}(\phi_{k}^{(i)})\|^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]

\displaystyle\quad+(1+\beta)\mathbb{E}[\|\nabla f_{\mathfrak{i}_{k+1}}(X_{k})-\nabla f_{\mathfrak{i}_{k+1}}(x_{\ast})-\nabla F_{N}(X_{k})\|^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]

\displaystyle\leq(1+\beta^{-1})\Big\{\mathbb{E}[\|\nabla f_{\mathfrak{i}_{k+1}}(\phi_{k}^{(\mathfrak{i}_{k+1})})-\nabla f_{\mathfrak{i}_{k+1}}(x_{\ast})\|^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]-\|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\nabla f_{i}(\phi_{k}^{(i)})\|^{2}\Big\}

\displaystyle\quad+(1+\beta)\left\{\mathbb{E}[\|\nabla f_{\mathfrak{i}_{k+1}}(X_{k})-\nabla f_{\mathfrak{i}_{k+1}}(x_{\ast})\|^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]-\|\nabla F_{N}(X_{k})\|^{2}\right\}

and all together

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\|\bar{G}_{k}\|^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]

\displaystyle\leq(1+\beta^{-1})\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\|\nabla f_{i}(\phi_{k}^{(i)})-\nabla f_{i}(x_{\ast})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+(1+\beta)\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\|\nabla f_{i}(X_{k})-\nabla f_{i}(x_{\ast})\|^{2}-\beta\|\nabla F_{N}(X_{k})\|^{2}\,.

∎

Applying the upper bound in Lemma
4.5.3
results in

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}[\|X_{k+1}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]&\leq\|X_{k}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}-2\bar{\alpha}\langle X_{k}-x_{\ast},\nabla F_{N}(X_{k})\rangle-\bar{\alpha}^{2}\beta\|\nabla F_{N}(X_{k})\|^{2}\\
&\quad+\bar{\alpha}^{2}(1+\beta^{-1})\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\|\nabla f_{i}(\phi_{k}^{(i)})-\nabla f_{i}(x_{\ast})\|^{2}\\
&\quad+\bar{\alpha}^{2}(1+\beta)\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\|\nabla f_{i}(X_{k})-\nabla f_{i}(x_{\ast})\|^{2}\,.\end{split}

(4.12)

With the following Lemma, we are able to control
\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\|\nabla f_{i}(X_{k})-\nabla f_{i}(x_{\ast})\|^{2}
by setting it in relation to
-\langle X_{k}-x_{\ast},\nabla F_{N}(X_{k})\rangle
.

{lemma}

Let
F_{N},\ f_{i}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
,
i=1,\dots,N
, be
\mu
-strongly convex and
L
-smooth. Then for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
it holds true that

\displaystyle\langle\nabla F_{N}(x),x_{\ast}-x\rangle

\displaystyle\leq-\frac{L-\mu}{L}(F_{N}(x)-F_{N}(x_{\ast}))-\frac{\mu}{2}\|x-x_{\ast}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{2L}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\|\nabla f_{i}(x)-\nabla f_{i}(x_{\ast})\|^{2}\,,

where
x_{\ast}=\arg\min_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\ F_{N}(x)
.

The proof of Lemma
4.5.3
is left as an exercise for the interested reader. We can now combine the upper bound in Lemma
4.5.3
with (
4.12
) to obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\|X_{k+1}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]

\displaystyle\leq(1-\bar{\alpha}\mu)\|X_{k}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}-2\bar{\alpha}\frac{L-\mu}{L}\left(F_{N}(X_{k})-F_{N}(x_{\ast})\right)-\bar{\alpha}^{2}\beta\|\nabla F_{N}(X_{k})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+(\bar{\alpha}^{2}(1+\beta)-\frac{\bar{\alpha}}{L})\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\|\nabla f_{i}(X_{k})-\nabla f_{i}(x_{\ast})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\bar{\alpha}^{2}(1+\beta^{-1})\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\|\nabla f_{i}(\phi_{k}^{(i)})-\nabla f_{i}(x_{\ast})\|^{2}

It remains to control

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\|\nabla f_{i}(\phi_{k}^{(i)})-\nabla f_{i}(x_{\ast})\|^{2}\,.

Since all
f_{i}
,
i=1,\dots,N
, are assumed to be
L
-smooth with the same
L>0
, we have that

\|\nabla f_{i}(\phi_{k}^{(i)})-\nabla f_{i}(x_{\ast})\|^{2}\leq 2L(f_{i}(\phi_{k}^{(i)})-f_{i}(x_{\ast})-\langle\nabla f_{i}(x_{\ast}),\phi_{k}^{(i)}-x_{\ast}\rangle)

(see also Lemma
4.5.4
below) implying that

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\|\nabla f_{i}(\phi_{k}^{(i)})-\nabla f_{i}(x_{\ast})\|^{2}\leq\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}f_{i}(\phi_{k}^{(i)})-F_{N}(x_{\ast})-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\langle\nabla f_{i}(x_{\ast}),\phi_{k}^{(i)}-x_{\ast}\rangle=E_{k}^{(1)},

which also explains the origin of the error function
E_{k}=E_{k}^{(2)}+E_{k}^{(1)}
. We are now ready to prove the linear convergence of SAGA.

{thm}
[Theorem 1 in
[
7
]
]
Let
F_{N},\ f_{i}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
,
i=1,\dots,N
, be
\mu
-strongly convex and
L
-smooth, and let
X_{0}
be a random variable such that
\mathbb{E}[E_{0}]=c\mathbb{E}[\|X_{0}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}]+\mathbb{E}[F_{N}(X_{0})-F_{N}(x_{\ast})]<\infty
. Moreover, let
(X_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be generated by Algorithm
10
with fixed step size
\alpha_{k}=\bar{\alpha}=\frac{1}{2(\mu N+L)}
. Then for
c=\frac{1}{2\bar{\alpha}(1-\bar{\alpha}\mu)N}
it holds true that

\mathbb{E}[E_{k+1}]\leq(1-\bar{\alpha}\mu)\mathbb{E}[E_{k}]\,.

Proof.

Firstly, we observe that each
\phi_{k+1}^{(i)}
given
\mathcal{F}_{k}
is distributed according to

\phi_{k+1}^{(i)}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}\sim\frac{1}{N}\delta_{X_{k}}+(1-\frac{1}{N})\delta_{\phi_{k}^{(i)}}\,,

such that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}[f_{i}(\phi_{k+1}^{(i)})\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(\frac{1}{N}f_{i}(X_{k})+(1-\frac{1}{N})f_{i}(\phi_{k}^{(i)})\right)=\frac{1}{N}F_{N}(X_{k})+(1-\frac{1}{N})\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}f_{i}(\phi_{k}^{(i)})\,.

With a similar computation we also obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\langle\nabla f_{i}(x_{\ast}),\phi_{k+1}^{(i)}-x_{\ast}\rangle]

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{N}\langle\nabla F_{N}(x_{\ast}),X_{k}-x_{\ast}\rangle-(1-\frac{1}{N})\sum_{i=1}^{N}\langle\nabla f_{i}(x_{\ast}),\phi_{k}^{(i)}-x_{\ast}\rangle

\displaystyle=-(1-\frac{1}{N})\sum_{i=1}^{N}\langle\nabla f_{i}(x_{\ast}),\phi_{k}^{(i)}-x_{\ast}\rangle\,.

Finally, we obtain the iterative error bound

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[E_{k+1}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{1}{N}-c2\bar{\alpha}\frac{L-\mu}{L}\right)\left(F_{N}(X_{k})-F_{N}(x_{\ast})\right)-c\bar{\alpha}^{2}\beta\|\nabla F_{N}(X_{K})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\left(1-\bar{\alpha}\mu\right)c\|X_{k}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}+\left(1-\frac{1}{N}+2c(1+\beta^{-1})\bar{\alpha}^{2}L-\bar{\alpha}\mu+\bar{\alpha}\mu\right)E_{k}^{(1)}

\displaystyle\quad+\left(c\bar{\alpha}(\bar{\alpha}(1+\beta)-\frac{1}{L}\right)\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\|\nabla f_{i}(X_{k})-\nabla f_{i}(x_{\ast})\|^{2}\,,

and using
-\|\nabla F_{N}(X_{K})\|^{2}\leq-2\mu(F_{N}(X_{k})-F_{N}(x_{\ast}))
by
\mu
-strong convexity of
F_{N}
yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[E_{k+1}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]

\displaystyle\leq(1-\bar{\alpha}\mu)E_{k}+\left(\frac{1}{N}-2c\bar{\alpha}\frac{L-\mu}{L}-2\mu\bar{\alpha}^{2}\beta c\right)\cdot\left(F_{N}(X_{k})-F_{N}(x_{\ast})\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\left(\bar{\alpha}\mu-\frac{1}{N}+2c(1+\beta^{-1})\bar{\alpha}^{2}L\right)E_{k}^{(1)}

\displaystyle\quad+c\bar{\alpha}(\bar{\alpha}(1+\beta)-\frac{1}{L})\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\|\nabla f_{i}(X_{k})-\nabla f_{i}(x_{\ast})\|^{2}\,,

With the choice
\bar{\alpha}=\frac{1}{2(\mu N+L)}
,
\beta=\frac{2\mu N+L}{L}
and
c=\frac{1}{2\bar{\alpha}(1-\bar{\alpha}\mu)N}
one can verify that

•

\bar{\alpha}(1+\beta)-\frac{1}{L}=0
,

•

\bar{\alpha}\mu-\frac{1}{N}+2c(1+\beta^{-1})\bar{\alpha}^{2}L\leq 0
,

•

\frac{1}{N}-2c\bar{\alpha}\frac{L-\mu}{L}-2\mu\bar{\alpha}^{2}\beta c=0
,

such that we conclude the proof with

\mathbb{E}[E_{k+1}]=\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{E}[E_{k+1}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]]\leq(1-\bar{\alpha}\mu)\mathbb{E}[E_{k}]\,.

∎

We observe that
c\mathbb{E}[\|X_{k}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}]\leq E_{k}
such that we obtain the following corollary:

{cor}

[Corollary 1 in
[
7
]
]
Under the same assumptions as in Theorem
4.5.3
it holds true that

\mathbb{E}[\|X_{k}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}]\leq\left(1-\frac{\mu}{2(\mu N+L)}\right)^{k}\left(\mathbb{E}[\|X_{0}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}+\frac{N}{\mu N+L}\mathbb{E}[F_{N}(X_{k})-F_{N}(x_{\ast})]\right)\,,

where
