x_{\ast}=\arg\min_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\ F_{N}(x)
.

Remark
\thethm
.

We emphasize again, that for both SAG and SAGA it is necessary to store
\{G_{k}^{(i)}\}_{i=1,\dots,N}
which may occur with additional cost.

In the following, we take a look at Table 1 of
[
43
]
(extended by the values for SAGA), where the theoretically derived rates of convergence of SAG (and SAGA) are compared to various deterministic first-order methods, which in each iteration need to evaluate the gradients across the entire data set
i=1,\dots,N
. We observe a significant improvement through SAG and SAGA.

and

.

4.5.4
Stochastic variance reduced gradient (SVRG)

In the following, we consider a variance reduction method for SGD that avoids storing gradients across the entire data set. The
stochastic variance reduced gradient
(SVRG) method has been introduced in
[
21
]
. The algorithm operates cyclic by a loop of SGD followed by an exact gradient update. Every
M
iterations, the scheme updates the gradient information across the entire data set. For each cycle of SGD followed by the exact gradient iteration, we need to evaluate
2M+N
gradients. In order to compare the algorithm to GD, SAG and SAGA, we will need to pay attention to this observation and rescale the effective rate of convergence accordingly.

1:

Input:

•

objective function
F_{N}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
,
F_{N}(x)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}f_{i}(x)

•

initial random variable
X_{0}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{d}

•

length of cycle
M\geq 1

•

sequence of step sizes
(\alpha_{k}^{(m)})_{k\in\mathbb{N},\ m=0,\dots,M-1}
,
\alpha_{k}^{(m)}>0
(deterministic or
\mathcal{F}
-adapted)

2:

set
k=0

3:

set
\tilde{X}_{0}=X_{0}^{(0)}
,
\tilde{G}_{0}=\nabla F_{N}(\tilde{X}_{0})

4:

set
X_{0}^{(0)}=X_{0}

\bar{G}_{0}^{(0)}=\tilde{G}_{0}

5:

while
”convergence/stopping criterion not met”
do

6:

for

m=0,\dots,M-1

do

7:

generate independently
\mathfrak{i}_{k+1}^{(m+1)}\sim\mathcal{U}(\{1,\dots,N\})

8:

approximate the gradient
\nabla F_{N}(X_{k}^{(m)})
through

\bar{G}_{k}^{(m)}=\nabla f_{\mathfrak{i}_{k+1}^{(m+1)}}(X_{k}^{(m)})-\nabla f_{\mathfrak{i}_{k+1}^{(m+1)}}(\tilde{X}_{k})+\tilde{G}_{k}

9:

set
X_{k}^{(m+1)}=X_{k}^{(m)}-\alpha_{k}^{(m)}\bar{G}_{k}^{(m)}
,

10:

end

for

11:

generate independently
\mathfrak{m}_{k+1}\sim\mathcal{U}(\{0,\dots,M-1\})
,

12:

set
\tilde{X}_{k+1}=X_{k}^{(\mathfrak{m}_{k+1})}
,

13:

set
\tilde{G}_{k+1}=\nabla F_{N}(\tilde{X}_{k+1})=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\nabla f_{i}(\tilde{X}_{k+1})

14:

set
X_{k+1}^{(0)}=\tilde{X}_{k+1}
,
\bar{G}_{k+1}^{(0)}=\tilde{G}_{k+1}

15:

set
k\mapsto k+1

16:

end

while

Algorithm 11

Stochastic variance reduced gradient method (SVRG)

We will follow the proof for linear convergence of SVRG presented in
[
21
]
. Let us start with the following auxiliary bound on the gradients
\nabla f_{i}
,
i=1,\dots,N
. Note that this result can be viewed as an extension of the inequality

\frac{1}{2L}\|\nabla F_{N}(x)-\nabla F_{N}(x_{\ast})\|^{2}\leq F_{N}(x)-F_{N}(x_{\ast}),

which we have derived under
L
-smoothness and convexity of
F_{N}
.

{lemma}

Let
F_{N},\ f_{i}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
,
i=1,\dots,N
, be convex and
L
-smooth. Then for
x_{\ast}=\arg\min_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\ F_{N}(x)
and all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
it holds

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\|\nabla f_{i}(x)-\nabla f_{i}(x_{\ast})\|^{2}\leq 2L\left(F_{N}(x)-F_{N}(x_{\ast})\right)\,.

Proof.

For each
i=1,\dots,N
, define the function

\varphi_{i}(x)=f_{i}(x)-f_{i}(x_{\ast})-\langle\nabla f_{i}(x_{\ast}),x-x_{\ast}\rangle\geq 0

such that
\nabla\varphi_{i}(x)=\nabla f_{i}(x)-\nabla f_{i}(x_{\ast})
and
\nabla\varphi_{i}(x_{\ast})=0
. Since
\varphi_{i}(x_{\ast})=0
and
\varphi_{i}(x)\geq 0
by convexity of
f_{i}
, we observe that
\varphi_{i}(x_{\ast})=\min_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\ \varphi_{i}(x)
. For any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
it holds that

\displaystyle 0=\varphi_{i}(x_{\ast})\leq\min_{\alpha\geq 0}\ \varphi_{i}(x-\alpha\nabla f_{i}(x))

\displaystyle\leq\min_{\alpha\geq 0}\ \left(\varphi_{i}(x)-\alpha(1-\frac{L}{2}\alpha)\|\nabla\varphi_{i}(x)\|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\varphi_{i}(x)-\frac{1}{2L}\|\nabla\varphi_{i}(x)\|^{2},

where we have used that
\varphi_{i}
is
L
-smooth (since
\nabla\varphi_{i}(x)=\nabla f_{i}(x)-\nabla f_{i}(x_{\ast})
remains
L
-Lipschitz continuous) and that
\max_{\alpha}\alpha(1-\frac{L}{2}\alpha)=\frac{1}{2L}
. Hence, it follows that

\|\nabla\varphi_{i}(x)\|^{2}=\|\nabla f_{i}(x)-\nabla f_{i}(x_{\ast})\|^{2}\leq 2L\varphi_{i}(x)=2L\left(f_{i}(x)-f_{i}(x_{\ast})-\langle\nabla f_{i}(x_{\ast}),x-x_{\ast}\rangle\right).

Taking the average over all
i=1,\dots,N
, finishes the proof

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\|\nabla f_{i}(x)-\nabla f_{i}(x_{\ast})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2L\left(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}f_{i}(x)-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}f_{i}(x_{\ast})-\langle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\nabla f_{i}(x_{\ast}),x-x_{\ast}\rangle\right)

\displaystyle=2L\left(F_{N}(x)-F_{N}(x_{\ast})\right)\,.

∎

Let
(X_{k}^{(m)})_{k\in\mathbb{N},\ m=0,\dots,M}
be generated by Algorithm
11
with fixed step size
\bar{\alpha}>0
and consider the following natural filtration

\mathcal{F}_{k}^{(m)}=\begin{cases}\sigma(\{X_{0}\},\{\mathfrak{i}_{\ell}^{(s)},\ell\leq k,s\leq M\},\{\mathfrak{i}_{k}^{(s)},s\leq m\},\{\mathfrak{m}_{\ell},\ell\leq k\}),&k\geq 1\\
\sigma(\{X_{0}\},\{\mathfrak{i}_{0}^{(s)},s\leq m\}),&k=0\end{cases}\,.

Similarly as in the case of SAGA, we have that

\mathbb{E}[\|X_{k}^{(m+1)}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}^{(m)}]=\|X_{k}^{(m)}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}-2\bar{\alpha}\langle X_{k}^{(m)}-x_{\ast},\nabla F(X_{k}^{(m)})\rangle+\bar{\alpha}^{2}\mathbb{E}[\|\bar{G}_{k}^{(m)}\|^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}^{(m)}],

since
\bar{G}_{k}^{(m)}
is an unbiased estimator of
\nabla F_{N}(X_{k}^{(m)})
:

\mathbb{E}[\bar{G}_{k}^{(m)}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}^{(m)}]=\mathbb{E}[\nabla f_{\mathfrak{i}_{k+1}^{(m+1)}}(X_{k}^{(m)})\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}^{(m)}]-\underbrace{\mathbb{E}[\nabla f_{\mathfrak{i}_{k+1}^{(m+1)}}(\tilde{X}_{k})-\tilde{G}_{k}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}^{(m)}]}_{=0}=\nabla F_{N}(X_{k}^{(m)})\,.

We again aim to control
\mathbb{E}[\|\bar{G}_{k}^{(m)}\|^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}^{(m)}]
in order to achieve significant variance reduction.

{lemma}

Let
F_{N},\ f_{i}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
,
i=1,\dots,N
, be
\mu
-strongly convex and
L
-smooth, and let
X_{0}
be a random variable such that
\mathbb{E}[\|X_{0}\|^{2}]+\mathbb{E}[|F_{N}(X_{0})|]<\infty
. Moreover, let
(X_{k}^{(m)})_{k\in\mathbb{N},m=0,\dots,M}
be generated by Algorithm
11
with fixed step size
\alpha_{k}^{(m)}=\bar{\alpha}>0
. Then for all
k\geq 0
and
m\geq 1
it holds true that

\mathbb{E}[\|\bar{G}_{k}^{(m)}\|^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}^{(m)}]\leq 4L\left(F_{N}(X_{k}^{(m)})-F_{N}(x_{\ast})+F_{N}(\tilde{X}_{k})-F_{N}(x_{\ast})\right)\,,

where
x_{\ast}=\arg\min_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\ F_{N}(x)
.

Proof.

The proof follows by similar argumentation as the proof of Lemma
4.5.3
. We again apply (
4.11
) together with
\|x+y\|^{2}\leq 2\|x\|^{2}+2\|y\|^{2}
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\|\bar{G}_{k}^{(m)}\|^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}^{(m)}]

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathbb{E}[\|\nabla f_{\mathfrak{i}_{k+1}^{(m+1)}}(X_{k}^{(m)})-\nabla f_{\mathfrak{i}_{k+1}^{(m+1)}}(x_{\ast})\|^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}^{(m)}]

\displaystyle\quad+2\mathbb{E}[\|\nabla f_{\mathfrak{i}_{k+1}^{(m+1)}}(\tilde{X}_{k})-\nabla f_{\mathfrak{i}_{k+1}^{(m+1)}}(x_{\ast})-\nabla F_{N}(\tilde{X}_{k})\|^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}^{(m)}]

\displaystyle=2\mathbb{E}[\|\nabla f_{\mathfrak{i}_{k+1}^{(m+1)}}(X_{k}^{(m)})-\nabla f_{\mathfrak{i}_{k+1}^{(m+1)}}(x_{\ast})\|^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}^{(m)}]

\displaystyle\quad+2\mathbb{E}[\|\{\nabla f_{\mathfrak{i}_{k+1}^{(m+1)}}(\tilde{X}_{k})-\nabla f_{\mathfrak{i}_{k+1}^{(m+1)}}(x_{\ast})\}-\{\nabla F_{N}(\tilde{X}_{k})-\nabla F_{N}(x_{\ast})\}\|^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}^{(m)}]

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathbb{E}[\|\nabla f_{\mathfrak{i}_{k+1}^{(m+1)}}(X_{k}^{(m)})-\nabla f_{\mathfrak{i}_{k+1}^{(m+1)}}(x_{\ast})\|^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}^{(m)}]

\displaystyle\quad+2\mathbb{E}[\|\nabla f_{\mathfrak{i}_{k+1}^{(m+1)}}(\tilde{X}_{k})-\nabla f_{\mathfrak{i}_{k+1}^{(m+1)}}(x_{\ast})\|^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}^{(m)}]

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(\|\nabla f_{i}(X_{k}^{(m)})-\nabla f_{i}(x_{\ast})\|^{2}+\|\nabla f_{i}(\tilde{X}_{k})-\nabla f_{i}(x_{\ast})\|^{2}\right)\,.

The assertion follows by application of Lemma
4.5.4
.
∎

We are now ready to prove linear convergence of SVRG under strong convexity.

{thm}
[Theorem 1 in
[
21
]
]
Let
F_{N},\ f_{i}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
,
i=1,\dots,N
, be
\mu
-strongly convex and
L
-smooth, and let
X_{0}
be a random variable such that
\mathbb{E}[\|X_{0}\|^{2}]+\mathbb{E}[|F_{N}(X_{0})|]<\infty
. Moreover, let
(X_{k}^{(m)})_{k\in\mathbb{N},\ m=0,\dots,M}
be generated by Algorithm
11
with fixed step size
\alpha_{k}^{(m)}=\bar{\alpha}>0
and
M\geq 1
such that

0<\rho:=\frac{1}{\mu(1-2\bar{\alpha}L)\bar{\alpha}M}+\frac{2\bar{\alpha}L}{1-2\bar{\alpha}L}<1\,.

Then it holds true that

\mathbb{E}[F_{N}(\tilde{X}_{k})-F_{N}(x_{\ast})]\leq\rho^{k}\mathbb{E}[F_{N}(X_{0})-F_{N}(x_{\ast})]\,.

Proof.

Using the update rule, the conditional unbiasedness of the SVRG estimator, and convexity of

F_{N}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\|X_{k}^{(m+1)}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}^{(m)}]

\displaystyle\leq\|X_{k}^{(m)}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}-2\bar{\alpha}\left(F_{N}(X_{k}^{(m)})-F_{N}(x_{\ast})\right)+\bar{\alpha}^{2}\mathbb{E}[\|\bar{G}_{k}^{(m)}\|^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}^{(m)}]

\displaystyle\leq\|X_{k}^{(m)}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}-2\bar{\alpha}\left(F_{N}(X_{k}^{(m)})-F_{N}(x_{\ast})\right)

\displaystyle\quad+4L\bar{\alpha}^{2}\left(F_{N}(X_{k}^{(m)})-F_{N}(x_{\ast})\right)+4L\bar{\alpha}^{2}\left(F_{N}(\tilde{X}_{k})-F_{N}(x_{\ast})\right)

\displaystyle=\|X_{k}^{(m)}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}-2\bar{\alpha}(1-2L\bar{\alpha})\left(F_{N}(X_{k}^{(m)})-F_{N}(x_{\ast})\right)

\displaystyle\quad+4L\bar{\alpha}^{2}\left(F_{N}(\tilde{X}_{k})-F_{N}(x_{\ast})\right)\,

where we have applied Lemma
4.5.4
. By construction of the Algorithm
11
we have

\mathbb{E}[F_{N}(\tilde{X}_{k+1})-F_{N}(x_{\ast})\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}^{(M)}]=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=0}^{M-1}\left(F_{N}(X_{k}^{(m)})-F_{N}(x_{\ast})\right),

such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\|X_{k}^{(M)}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}]+2\bar{\alpha}(1-2L\bar{\alpha})M\mathbb{E}[F_{N}(\tilde{X}_{k+1})-F_{N}(x_{\ast})]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}[\|X_{k}^{(0)}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}]-2\bar{\alpha}(1-2L\bar{\alpha})\sum_{m=0}^{M-1}\mathbb{E}[F_{N}(X_{k}^{(m)})-F_{N}(x_{\ast})]

\displaystyle\quad+2\bar{\alpha}(1-2L\bar{\alpha})M\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=0}^{M-1}\mathbb{E}[F_{N}(X_{k}^{(m)})-F_{N}(x_{\ast})]

\displaystyle\quad+4L\bar{\alpha}^{2}M\mathbb{E}[F_{N}(\tilde{X}_{k})-F_{N}(x_{\ast})]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{\mu}\mathbb{E}[F_{N}(X_{k}^{(0)})-F_{N}(x_{\ast})]+4L\bar{\alpha}^{2}M\mathbb{E}[F_{N}(\tilde{X}_{k})-F_{N}(x_{\ast})]

\displaystyle=2(\frac{1}{\mu}+2L\bar{\alpha}^{2}M)\mathbb{E}[F_{N}(\tilde{X}_{k})-F_{N}(x_{\ast})]\,,

where we have used that
\frac{\mu}{2}\|x-x_{\ast}\|^{2}\leq F_{N}(x)-F_{N}(x_{\ast})
by strong convexity of
F_{N}
. Finally, we have

\mathbb{E}[F_{N}(\tilde{X}_{k+1})-F_{N}(x_{\ast})]\leq\left(\frac{1}{\mu(1-2\bar{\alpha}L)\bar{\alpha}M}+\frac{2\bar{\alpha}L}{1-2\bar{\alpha}L}\right)\mathbb{E}[F_{N}(\tilde{X}_{k})-F_{N}(x_{\ast})]\,.

∎

Remark
\thethm
.

Returning to the setting of Table
4.1
. For
L=100
,
\mu=0.01
and
N=10^{5}=10\kappa
, we want to choose
\bar{\alpha}=\frac{\tau}{L}
,
\tau\leq\frac{1}{2}
such that
\rho
defined in Theorem
4.5.4
is given by

\rho=\frac{1}{1-2\tau}\left(\frac{\kappa}{\tau M}+2\tau\right)=\frac{1}{1-2\tau}\left(\frac{N}{10\tau M}+2\tau\right)\,.

We set
\tau=\frac{1}{10}
,
M=4N
such that
\rho=\frac{9}{16}
. Since one SVRG outer iteration requires
2M+N
component gradient evaluations, while one full
gradient descent step requires
N
component gradient evaluations, the effective contraction factor
per cost of one full gradient evaluation is

\rho^{\mathrm{SVRG}}=\rho^{\frac{N}{2M+N}}=\left(\frac{9}{16}\right)^{\frac{1}{9}}\approx 0.94,

which still achieves a better convergence rate than GD. Note that the above choice of step size has been chosen heuristically and is not optimized.

The three finite-sum methods illustrate different ways of reducing variance. SAG and SAGA store past component gradients and therefore require additional memory of order
Nd
. SVRG avoids this storage cost by using occasional full-gradient computations at snapshot points. In all cases, the goal is to recover linear convergence in strongly convex finite-sum problems while using mostly stochastic updates.

4.6
Further reading and outlook

Stochastic gradient methods date back to the seminal work of Robbins and Monro
[
41
]
and
are now among the standard algorithms for large-scale optimization and machine learning. As mentioned earlier, for a broad introduction to stochastic approximation and stochastic gradient methods, we refer to
Bottou et al.
[
4
]
and to the recent handbook of convergence proofs by Garrigos and Gower
[
13
]
.

The convergence results in Section
4.2
and
4.3
are classical in spirit. Proofs of the convergence rates in expectation for SGD under smoothness and convexity assumptions can be found, for example, in
[
34
,
36
,
38
]
. For smooth but non-convex objectives, the standard goal is to prove convergence of the (minimal, averaged or randomly selected) gradient norm; see
[
15
]
. For the non-smooth but convex setting we refer to
[
27
]
.

Almost sure convergence has a long history in stochastic approximation. One fundamental tool is the Robbins–Siegmund supermartingale convergence theorem
[
40
]
, which we discussed in Section
4.2
. Other approaches to verify almost sure convergence guarantees for SGD are described in
[
1
,
3
,
5
,
8
,
36
,
33
]
. Almost sure convergence rates have received renewed attention in recent years. In particular, Sebbouh et al.
[
44
]
and Liu and Yuan
[
30
]
derive almost sure rates for stochastic gradient methods in smooth (convex and non-convex) settings. They also treat stochastic momentum such as heavy ball and Nesterov acceleration.

Another important line of work replaces convexity by local or global gradient domination conditions, such as the PL condition (
2.16
) or the weaker condition (
2.17
). These conditions are weaker than strong convexity but can still imply convergence to global minimizers. For convergence results in expectation under gradient domination, we refer to

[
10
,
11
,
22
,
23
]
.
Almost sure (last iterate) convergence rates for SGD under global and local gradient domination are studied in
[
46
]
.

Several important topics are only briefly touched upon in these notes. These include stochastic
momentum methods, adaptive step size rules such as AdaGrad
[
9
]
, AdaDelta
[
49
]
, RMSProp
[
45
]
and Adam
[
25
]
, high-probability bounds
[
31
]
,
and local convergence theory near non-isolated minima or saddle points
[
33
]
. For stochastic momentum
methods, we refer to recent analyzes such as
[
12
,
14
,
30
,
32
,
44
]
.

Appendix A
Appendix

A.1
Convex sets and functions

We give a brief overview of convex functions and properties that are important for optimization. This appendix covers selected material from
[
2
, Appendix B]
, adapted to
the notation and scope of these notes. We start with the following basic definition.

{defi}
[convex function]
A subset
C\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is called
convex
, if

\lambda x+(1-\lambda)y\in C

for all
x,y\in C
and
\lambda\in[0,1]
.
Let
C\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a convex set. A function
f:C\to\mathbb{R}
is called
convex function
, if

f(\lambda x+(1-\lambda)y)\leq\lambda f(x)+(1-\lambda)f(y)

for all
x,y\in C
and
\lambda\in[0,1]
. A function
f
is called concave, if
-f
is convex. Moreover, a function
f
is called strictly convex, if

f(\lambda x+(1-\lambda)y)<\lambda f(x)+(1-\lambda)f(y)

for all
x,y\in C
,
x\neq y
and
\lambda\in(0,1)
.

Example
\thethm
.

Consider the following examples.

1.

Let
\{C_{i},\ i\in I\}
be a family of convex sets. Then
\cap_{i\in I}C_{i}
is a convex set.

2.

Let
C_{1},C_{2}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be two convex sets, then the set

C=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\mid x=x_{1}+x_{2},\ x_{1}\in C_{1},x_{2}\in C_{2}\}

is a convex set.

3.

The image of a convex set under linear transformation is again a convex set.

4.

Let
C\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a convex set and let
f:C\to\mathbb{R}
be a convex function. Then the level sets

A_{\alpha}=\{x\in C\mid f(x)\leq\alpha\}\quad\text{and}\quad B_{\alpha}=\{x\in C\mid f(x)<\alpha\}

are convex sets for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
.

{defi}

Let
f:C\to\mathbb{R}
be a function and
C\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a convex set. We define the
epigraph
of
f
as

\mathcal{E}(f):=\{(x,w)\in C\times\mathbb{R}\mid f(x)\leq w\},

the set of all points above the
graph
of
f
defined as

\mathcal{G}(f):=\{(x,w)\in C\times\mathbb{R}\mid f(x)=w\}.

We can characterize convex functions via convexity of its epigraph.

{prop}
[Fact]
Let
f:C\to\mathbb{R}
be a function and
C\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a convex set. The
f
is convex if and only if its epigraph
\mathcal{E}(f)
is a convex set.

Figure A.1:
Illustration of the connection between the graph
\mathcal{G}(f)
, the epigraph
\mathcal{E}(f)
and the convexity of
f
.

Next, we describe an inequality for convex functions which will be used often times in this lecture.

{prop}
[Jensen’s inequality]
Let
f:C\to\mathbb{R}
be a convex function,
C\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a convex set and
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n}\in(0,1)
with
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}=1
. Then it holds true that

f(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}x_{i})\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}f(x_{i}).

{exercise}

Prove Proposition
A.1
.

One typical example for the application of Jensen’s inequality is

\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\right)^{2}\leq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{2}.

Example
\thethm
.

In the following we present a row of useful properties in the context of convex functions.

1.

Every linear function is convex.

2.

Every norm in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is convex.

3.

Let
f_{1},\dots,f_{n}
be convex functions and
c_{1},\dots,c_{n}\geq 0
.

a)

Then
x\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}(x)
is a convex function.

b)

Then
x\mapsto\sup_{i=1,\dots,n}\ f_{i}(x)
is a convex function.

{exercise}

Prove the properties presented in Example
A.1
.

{prop}

Let
C\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a convex set and let
f:C\to\mathbb{R}
be differentiable over
C
. Then the following holds true:

1.

The function
f
is convex over
C
if and only if

f(z)\geq f(x)+(z-x)^{\top}\nabla f(x),

for all
x,z\in C
.

2.

If

f(z)>f(x)+(z-x)^{\top}\nabla f(x),\quad x\neq z,

for all
x,z\in C
, then
f
is strictly convex.

Proof.

We only prove the first assertion, the second one follows by similar argumentation. Firstly, assume that
f
is convex and let
x,z\in C
. Since
C
is a convex set,
x+a(z-x)\in C
for all
a\in(0,1)
. The convexity of
f
implies that

f(x+a(z-x))\leq af(z)+(1-a)f(x),

which we can rewrite to

\frac{f(x+a(z-x))-f(x)}{a}\leq f(z)-f(x).

On the other side, since
f
is differentiable, we have

(z-x)^{\top}\nabla f(x)=\lim_{a\leftharpoondown 0}\frac{f(x+a(z-x))-f(x)}{a}\leq f(z)-f(x).

This proves the first direction
"\Rightarrow"
.
Secondly, for the direction
"\Leftarrow"
suppose that

f(z)\geq f(x)+(z-x)^{\top}\nabla f(x)

(A.1)

for all
x,z\in C
. We fix arbitrary
x,y\in C
and
\lambda\in(0,1)
, and define
z:=\lambda x+(1-\lambda)y
. Then by (
A.1
) we have both

\displaystyle f(x)

\displaystyle\geq f(z)+(x-z)^{\top}\nabla f(z)

\displaystyle f(y)

\displaystyle\geq f(z)+(y-z)^{\top}\nabla f(z).

Combining both inequalities yields

\displaystyle\lambda f(x)+(1-\lambda)f(y)

\displaystyle\geq\lambda f(z)+\lambda(x-z)^{\top}\nabla f(z)+(1-\lambda)f(z)+(1-\lambda)(y-z)^{\top}\nabla f(z)

\displaystyle=f(z)+(\lambda x+(1-\lambda)y-z)\nabla f(z)

\displaystyle=f(z)=f(\lambda x+(1-\lambda)y).

Since
x,y\in C
are arbitrary, we have proven convexity of
f
.
∎

{prop}

Let
C\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a convex set,
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
twice continuous differentiable over
C
and let
Q\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
be a symmetric matrix.

1.

If
\nabla^{2}f(x)
is positive semi-definite for all
x\in C
, then
f
is convex over
C
.

2.

If
\nabla^{2}f(x)
is positive definite for all
x\in C
, then
f
is strictly convex over
C
.

3.

If
C=\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
f
is convex, then
\nabla^{2}f(x)
is positive semi-definite for all
x\in C
.

4.

The quadratic function
x\mapsto f(x)=x^{\top}Qx
is convex if and only if
Q
is positive semi-definite. Moreover,
f
is strictly convex if and only if
Q
is positive definite.

Proof.

1.

Let
x,y\in C
, then by the mean-value theorem there exists
\alpha\in[0,1]
such that

\displaystyle f(y)

\displaystyle=f(x)+(y-x)^{\top}\nabla f(x)+\frac{1}{2}(y-x)^{\top}\nabla^{2}f(x+\alpha(y-x))(y-x)

\displaystyle\geq f(x)+(y-x)^{\top}f(x),

where we have used that
\nabla^{2}f(x+\alpha(y-x))
is positive semi-definite, since
x+\alpha(y-x)\in C
. Then
f
is convex by Proposition
A.1
.

2.

Follows similarly as the proof of the first claim.

3.

Let
f
be convex and suppose that there exists
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
z^{\top}\nabla^{2}f(x)z<0
for some
z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. By assumption we have that
x\mapsto\nabla^{2}f(x)
is continuous, such that for
\bar{\alpha}>0
small enough it holds true that

z^{\top}\nabla^{2}f(x)z<0,\quad\text{for}\ \alpha\in[0,\bar{\alpha}).

For an arbitrary
\tilde{\alpha}\in[0,\bar{\alpha})
we set
\tilde{z}=\tilde{\alpha}z
. By the mean-value theorem there exists
\beta\in[0,1]
such that

\displaystyle f(x+\tilde{z})

\displaystyle=f(x)+\tilde{z}^{\top}\nabla f(x)+\frac{1}{2}\tilde{z}^{\top}\nabla^{2}f(x+\beta\tilde{z})\tilde{z}

\displaystyle=f(x)+\tilde{z}^{\top}\nabla f(x)+\frac{1}{2}\tilde{\alpha}^{2}z^{\top}\nabla f(x+\underbrace{\beta\tilde{\alpha}}_{<\bar{\alpha}}z)z

\displaystyle<f(x)+\tilde{z}^{\top}\nabla f(x),

which is in contradiction to the convexity of
f
(see Proposition
A.1
).

4.

The hessian of
f
is given by
\nabla^{2}f(x)=2Q
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. With 1. and 3. it follows that
f
is convex if and only if
Q
is positive semi-definite. If
Q
is positive definite, then convexity of
f
follows by the 3. assertion. It is left to prove that strict convexity of
f
implies that
Q
is positive definite. Assume that
f
is strictly convex, then by 3. we know that
Q
is positive semi-definite. If a matrix
A\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
is positive semi-definite but not positive definite, then there exists at least one
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
x\neq 0
such that
x^{\top}Ax=0
, i.e.
Ax=0=0\cdot x
. Hence, to prove that
Q
is positive definite we will show that
\lambda=0
is no eigenvalue of
Q
. Suppose
\lambda=0
is an eigenvalue of
Q
, then there exists
x\neq 0
such that
Qx=\lambda x=0
. This implies that

\frac{1}{2}f(x)+\frac{1}{2}f(-x)=\frac{1}{2}(x^{\top}Qx+(-x)^{\top}Q(-x)=x^{\top}Qx=0=f(\frac{1}{2}x-\frac{1}{2}x),

which is in contradiction to strict convexity of
f
. Hence,
Q
is positive definite.

∎

We will often consider the class of strong convex functions.

{defi}
[strongly convex function]
Let
C\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a convex set. A function
f:C\to\mathbb{R}
is called
\mu
-strongly convex
over
C
, if

f(y)\geq f(x)+(y-x)^{\top}\nabla f(x)+\frac{\mu}{2}\|y-x\|^{2}

for all
x,y\in C
.

{prop}

1.

Every
\mu
-strongly convex function
f
is also strictly convex.

2.

A function
f:C\to\mathbb{R}
is
\mu
-strongly convex if and only if its hessian
\nabla^{2}f(x)
is uniformly positive definite with

z^{\top}\nabla^{2}f(x)z\geq\mu\|z\|^{2}

for all
x\in C
and all
z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

{exercise}

Prove Proposition
A.1
.

A.2
Optimality conditions

We recall a few basic optimality conditions that are used throughout the notes. The selection and
presentation are partly guided by
[
2
, Section 1.1]
, with the notation adapted to the rest of these notes. We start with the necessary optimality conditions of first-order, which only needs differentiability of the objective function.

{thm}

Let
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be continuously differentiable over the open set
S\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
and suppose
x_{\ast}\in S
is a local minimum of
f
. Then it holds true that
\nabla f(x_{\ast})=0
.

Proof.

Let
x_{\ast}\in S
be a local minimum of
f
and consider the corresponding ball
\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}(x_{\ast})
such that
f(x_{\ast})\leq f(x)
for all
x\in\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}(x_{\ast})
. Let
z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
be arbitrary but fixed, such that for
\bar{\alpha}>0
small enough
x_{\ast}+\alpha z\in\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}(x_{\ast})
and hence
f(x_{\ast}+\alpha z)\geq f(x_{\ast})
for all
\alpha\in[0,\bar{\alpha})
. We define
\alpha\mapsto g(\alpha)=f(x_{\ast}+\alpha z)
,
\alpha>0
and observe

\frac{{\mathrm{d}}g(0)}{d\alpha}=\lim_{\alpha\to 0}\frac{f(x_{\ast}+\alpha z)-f(x_{\ast})}{\alpha}\geq 0.

On the other side by chain rule we also have

\frac{{\mathrm{d}}g(0)}{{\mathrm{d}}\alpha}=z^{\top}\nabla f(x_{\ast}+\alpha z).

In particular, this implies
0\leq z^{\top}\nabla f(x_{\ast})
. Since
z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is arbitrary, with
y=-z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we also obtain
0\leq y^{\top}\nabla f(x_{\ast})=-z^{\top}\nabla f(x_{\ast})
. Therefore, for all
z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have
z^{\top}\nabla f(x_{\ast})=0
, which yields
\nabla f(x_{\ast})=0
.
∎

Under the additional assumption that
f
is twice continuously differentiable there is also a necessary optimality condition of second-order.

{thm}

Let
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be twice continuously differentiable over the open set
S\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
and suppose
x_{\ast}\in S
is a local minimum of
f
. Then it holds true that
\nabla^{2}f(x_{\ast})
is positive semi-definite.

Proof.

Let again
x_{\ast}\in S
be the local minimum of
f
with the corresponding ball
\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}(x_{\ast})
such that
f(x_{\ast})\leq f(x)
for all
x\in\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}(x_{\ast})
and consider an arbitrary
z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. With the Taylor expansion it holds

f(x_{\ast}+\alpha z)-f(x_{\ast})=\alpha\underbrace{\nabla f(x_{\ast})^{\top}}_{=0}z+\frac{\alpha^{2}}{2}z^{\top}\nabla^{2}f(x_{\ast})z+o(\alpha^{2})=\frac{\alpha^{2}}{2}z^{\top}\nabla^{2}f(x_{\ast})z+o(\alpha^{2})\,.

Let
\bar{\alpha}>0
be small enough such that
f(x_{\ast}+\alpha z)\geq f(x_{\ast})
for all
\alpha\in[0,\bar{\alpha})
. With the above equation we obtain

0\leq\frac{f(x_{\ast}+\alpha z)-f(x_{\ast})}{\alpha^{2}}=\frac{1}{2}z^{\top}\nabla^{2}f(x_{\ast})z+\frac{o(\alpha^{2})}{\alpha^{2}}\to\frac{1}{2}z^{\top}\nabla^{2}f(x_{\ast})z

for
\alpha\to 0
. Since
z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is chosen arbitrary, this leads to
z^{\top}\nabla^{2}f(x_{\ast})z\geq 0
for all
z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and the assertion follows.
∎

Remark
\thethm
.

Theorem
A.2
and
A.2
characterize necessary, but not sufficient, conditions for optimality. Firstly, consider for example
f(x)=-x^{2}
with stationary point
x_{\ast}=0
, which is no local minimum. Secondly, consider
f(x)=x_{1}^{2}-x_{2}^{4}
,
x=(x_{1},x_{2})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
with stationary point
x_{\ast}=(0,0)^{\top}
and positive semi-definite Hessian
\nabla^{2}f(x_{\ast})=\begin{pmatrix}2&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}
, which is no local minimum.

In the following theorem, we formulate second-order sufficient conditions for optimality.

{thm}

Let
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be twice continuously differentiable over the open set
S\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Let
x_{\ast}\in S
with

1.

\nabla f(x_{\ast})=0

2.

\nabla^{2}f(x_{\ast})
(strictly) positive definite.

Then
x_{\ast}
is a strict local minimum of
f
and there exist
\gamma>0
,
\varepsilon>0
such that

f(x)\geq f(x_{\ast})+\frac{\gamma}{2}\|x-x_{\ast}\|^{2}

for all
x\in\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}(x_{\ast})
.

Before proving Theorem
A.2
, we will need to prove the following auxiliary result.

{lemma}

Let
A\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
be a symmetric matrix with real valued eigenvalues
\lambda_{1}\leq\dots\leq\lambda_{d}
and corresponding eigenvectors
v_{1},\dots,v_{d}
. Then it holds true that:

1.

\lambda_{1}\|z\|^{2}\leq z^{\top}Az\leq\lambda_{d}\|z\|^{2}
for all
z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

2.

The matrix
A
is (strict) positive definite if and only if all eigenvalues are (strictly) positive.

Proof.

We start with the first assertion. Let
z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and write

z=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\xi_{i}v_{i}

with coefficients
\xi_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
. Then we can write

\displaystyle z^{\top}Az=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\xi_{i}^{2}\langle v_{i},\underbrace{Av_{i}}_{=\lambda_{i}v_{i}}\rangle=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\lambda_{i}\xi_{i}^{2}\|v_{i}\|^{2}\begin{cases}\geq&\lambda_{1}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\xi_{i}^{2}\|v_{i}\|^{2}=\lambda_{i}\|z\|^{2},\\
\leq&\lambda_{d}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\xi_{i}^{2}\|v_{i}\|^{2}=\lambda_{d}\|z\|^{2}.,\end{cases}

which finishes the proof of the first claim.
For the second assertion we start with ”
\Rightarrow
”. Let
(\lambda_{i},v_{i})
be eigenvalue and corresponding eigenvector of the (strictly) positive definite matrix
A
. By definition of a positive definite matrix we have

0\leq(<)v_{i}^{\top}Av_{i}=v_{i}^{\top}(\lambda_{i}v_{i})=\lambda_{i}\|v_{i}\|^{2}.

Since
\|v_{i}\|^{2}>0
for
v_{i}\neq 0
, we obtain
\lambda_{i}\geq(>)0
for all
i=1,\dots,d
.
For the other way around ”
\Leftarrow
”, we assume that
0\leq(<)\lambda_{1}\leq\dots\leq\lambda_{d}
are eigenvalues of
A
with corresponding eigenvectors
v_{1}\dots,v_{d}
. Then it follows with the first assertion

z^{\top}Az\geq\lambda_{1}\|z\|^{2}\geq(>)0

for all
z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
z\neq 0
.
∎

We are now ready to prove Theorem
A.2
.

Proof of Theorem
A.2
.

Let
\lambda>0
be the smallest eigenvalue of the positive definite matrix
\nabla^{2}f(x_{\ast})
. By Lemma
A.2
it holds true that

z^{\top}\nabla^{2}f(x_{\ast})z\geq\lambda\|z\|^{2}.

Application of Taylor’s expansion around
x_{\ast}
together with
\nabla f(x_{\ast})=0
yields

\displaystyle f(x_{\ast}+d)-f(x_{\ast})

\displaystyle=\nabla f(x_{\ast})^{\top}d+\frac{1}{2}d^{\top}\nabla^{2}f(x_{\ast})d+o(\|d\|^{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}d^{\top}\nabla^{2}f(x_{\ast})d+o(\|d\|^{2})

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\lambda}{2}\|d\|^{2}+o(\|d\|^{2})

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\lambda}{2}+\frac{o(\|d\|^{2})}{\|d\|^{2}}\right)\|d\|^{2}.

Let
\varepsilon>0
be sufficiently small such that it holds
\frac{o(\|d\|^{2})}{\|d\|^{2}}\in(-\frac{\lambda}{4},\frac{\lambda}{4})
for
\|d\|^{2}<\varepsilon
. For
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\|x-x_{\ast}\|<\varepsilon
it follows

f(x)\geq f(x_{\ast})+\left(\frac{\lambda}{2}+\frac{o(\|x-x_{\ast}\|^{2})}{\|x-x_{\ast}\|^{2}}\right)\|x-x_{\ast}\|^{2}\geq f(x_{\ast})+\underbrace{(\frac{\lambda}{2}-\frac{\lambda}{4})}_{=:\frac{\gamma}{2}}\|x-x_{\ast}\|^{2}>f(x_{\ast}).

∎

Under the additional assumption of a convex objective function
f
, we can further characterize sufficient optimality conditions. The proof of the following proposition is left as an exercise for the interested reader.

{prop}

Let
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be continuously differentiable and convex over the convex set
S\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then it holds true that:

1.

Every local minimum of
f
over
S
is also a global minimum over
S
.

2.

If
f
is even strictly convex, then there exists at most one global minimum.

3.

Let
S
be an open set. Then the condition
\nabla f(x_{\ast})=0
is a sufficient and necessary condition for
x_{\ast}\in S
to be a global minimum of
f
over
S
.

A.3
Lyapunov methods for optimization

We will motivate the application of Lyapunov theory to optimization methods based on stability analysis of ordinary differential equations (ODEs). Lyapunov theory can be applied to analyze the behavior of dynamical systems without solving them analytically. Let

\frac{{\mathrm{d}}z(t)}{{\mathrm{d}}t}=g(z(t)),\quad z(0)=z_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

(A.2)

be a continuous-time dynamical system described as ODE with
g:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
locally Lipschitz-continuous.

{defi}

We call a point
\bar{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

equilibrium point
of (
A.2
) if
g(\bar{z})=0
.

Lyapunov methods can be applied to describe stability of equilibrium points of (
A.2
). Without loss of generality we assume that
\bar{z}=0\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is an equilibrium point of
(
A.2
)
.

{defi}

The equilibrium point
\bar{z}=0
is called

1.

stable
, if for all
\varepsilon>0
there exists a
\delta=\delta(\varepsilon)>0
such that:

\|z(0)\|<\delta\quad\implies\quad\|z(t)\|<\varepsilon\quad\text{for all}\ t\geq 0.

2.

unstable
, if
\bar{z}
is not stable.

3.

locally asymptotically stable
, if
\bar{z}
is stable and
\delta>0
can be chosen such that:

\|z(0)\|<\delta\quad\implies\quad\lim_{t\to\infty}z(t)=0.

4.

globally stable
, if
\bar{z}
is stable and
\lim_{\varepsilon\to\infty}\delta(\varepsilon)=\infty
.

5.

globally asymptotically stable
, if
\lim_{t\to\infty}z(t)=0
for all
z_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

We consider a continuously differentiable function
V:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
- in the following called candidate of a Lyapunov function - which will be used to be described along the trajectories. With the help of chain rule, we can describe the dynamical behavior of
V
along the trajectory of
z(t)
:

\frac{{\mathrm{d}}V(z(t))}{{\mathrm{d}}t}=\langle\nabla_{z}V(z(t)),\frac{{\mathrm{d}}z(t)}{{\mathrm{d}}t}\rangle=\langle\nabla_{z}V(z(t)),g(z(t))\rangle.

Under specific assumptions on the function
V
one can verify global stability of
\bar{z}
.

{thm}
[Theorem 3.2 in
[
24
]
]
Let
\bar{z}=0
be an equilibrium point of (
A.2
). Moreover, let
V:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be continuously differentiable with

1.

V(\bar{z})=0
and
V(z)>0
for all
z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{\bar{z}\}
,

2.

V(z)\to\infty
for
\|z\|\to\infty
,

3.

\frac{{\mathrm{d}}V(z(t))}{{\mathrm{d}}t}=\langle\nabla_{z}V(z(t)),g(z(t))\rangle\leq-W(z(t))
, for some continuous function
W:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
.

If
W(z)\geq 0
for all
z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, then
\bar{z}
is globally stable. Moreover, if
W(z)>0
for all
z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{\bar{z}\}
, then
\bar{z}
is even globally asymptotically stable.

A function satisfying the above conditions is sometimes also referred to a
Lyapunov function
.

We want to apply Lyapunov theory as tool for analyzing convergence of optimization methods. Let us start with an motivating example in continuous-time.

Example
\thethm
.

The gradient descent method with fixed step size
\bar{\alpha}>0
is written as

x_{k+1}=x_{k}-\bar{\alpha}\nabla f(x_{k}),

which for
\bar{\alpha}\to 0
can be interpreted as Euler-scheme of the ODE

\frac{{\mathrm{d}}x(t)}{{\mathrm{d}}t}=-\nabla f(x(t)).

(A.3)

In general, optimization schemes are often described and/or analysed through its continuous-time formulation. The system is sometimes also called gradient flow and intuitively speaking, it describes gradient descent with degenerated step size. It can be used as indicator of how gradient descent may perform with sufficiently small step size
\bar{\alpha}>0
. We have analysed gradient descent methods under various settings such as (strong) convexity and smoothness. The convergence analysis of the gradient flow (
A.3
) can be done in a very similar way using Lyapunov theory. We want to construct a function describing the convergence behavior of the dynamical system (
A.3
) without solving it explicitly. The most straightforward analysis can be done for the error function

V(x(t))=\mathcal{E}(x(t))=f(x(t))-f^{\ast}
, where
f^{\ast}=\min_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\ f(x)>-\infty
. Similar as before, we can describe the dynamical behavior of
V
through the ODE

\frac{{\mathrm{d}}V(x(t))}{{\mathrm{d}}t}=\langle\nabla_{x}V(x(t)),\frac{{\mathrm{d}}x(t)}{{\mathrm{d}}t}\rangle=-\langle\nabla f(x(t)),\nabla f(x(t))\rangle=-\|\nabla f(x(t))\|^{2}.

Under suitable conditions on
f
one can now apply Theorem
A.3
in order to quantify stability of a (unique) stationary point
x_{\ast}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
of
f
as equilibrium point of the gradient flow (
A.3
).

Let us consider a optimization scheme in continuous-time of the form

\frac{{\mathrm{d}}x(t)}{{\mathrm{d}}t}=g(x(t)),

(A.4)

and a corresponding error function
\mathcal{E}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
to be analyzed. We aim to quantify the convergence of (
A.4
) through the error function along the trajectory

\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{E}(x(t))}{{\mathrm{d}}t}=\langle\nabla_{x}\mathcal{E}(x(t)),g(x(t))\rangle\begin{cases}\leq 0\\
<0\end{cases}\,.

This can be used to obtain results such as monotonically decreasing error (
\leq 0
) or even convergence of the error (
<0
). However, these properties are not sufficient to describe the speed of convergence. In order to say something about the convergence speed, we can define a time-dependent error function
\widehat{\mathcal{E}}:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
of the form

\widehat{\mathcal{E}}(t,x)=\gamma(t)\mathcal{E}(x),

where
\gamma:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
(smooth and continuously differentiable) is devoted to describe the speed of convergence. Let us illustrate the strategy of proving convergence with the help of this error function throught the following example.

Example
\thethm
.

We want to minimize
f
using (
A.4
) and prove convergence of the error function
\mathcal{E}(x)=f(x)-f^{\ast}
. One possible strategy is to construct the time dependent error function

\widehat{\mathcal{E}}(t,x(t))=\gamma(t)\mathcal{E}(x(t))+r(x(t)),

where
\gamma:[0,\infty)
with
\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\gamma(t)}{{\mathrm{d}}t}>0
describes our guess of convergence rate and
r:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is an auxiliary function with
r(x)\geq 0
. Suppose we are able to prove monotonicity of the error in the form of

\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\widehat{\mathcal{E}}(t,x(t))}{{\mathrm{d}}t}\leq 0

(using again chain rule), we can directly imply by definition of
\widehat{\mathcal{E}}
that

\widehat{\mathcal{E}}(t,x(t))=\gamma(t)(f(x(t))-f^{\ast})+r(x(t))\leq\widehat{\mathcal{E}}(0,x(0)),

and therefore, we can imply convergence of the error
\mathcal{E}
in the form of

\mathcal{E}(x(t))=f(x(t))-f^{\ast}\leq\frac{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}(0,x(0))}{\gamma(t)}.

A similar strategy for discrete-time optimization schemes is described in Section
3.3.1
.

A.4
Measure-theoretic background

In the following section, we will briefly recall the definition of Dynkin systems and
\sigma
-algebras, and a useful tool which allows to prove measure theoretical properties on a
\cap
-stable generator of a
\sigma
-algebra. For example, if one wants to verify that two measures
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
on the same measurable space
(\Omega,\mathcal{A})
are equal, it is sufficient to verify the equality on an
\cap
-stable generator
\mathcal{E}
of
\mathcal{A}
(i.e.
\sigma(\mathcal{E})=\mathcal{A}
). For more details we refer to
[
26
, Section 1]
.

{defi}

Let
\mathcal{A}\subset\mathcal{P}(\Omega)
be a non-empty family of subsets of
\Omega
. We call
\mathcal{A}
a
\sigma
-algebra
if

1.

\Omega\in\mathcal{A}
,

2.

A\in\mathcal{A}

\implies

A^{\complement}\in\mathcal{A}
,

3.

A_{1},A_{2},\dots\in\mathcal{A}

\implies

\bigcup\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}A_{i}\in\mathcal{A}

{defi}

Let
\mathcal{D}\subset\mathcal{P}(\Omega)
be a non-empty family of subsets of
\Omega
. We call
\mathcal{D}
a
Dynkin system
if

1.

\Omega\in\mathcal{D}
,

2.

A\in\mathcal{D}

\implies

A^{\complement}\in\mathcal{D}
,

3.

A_{1},A_{2},\dots\in\mathcal{D}
pairwise disjoint (i.e.
A_{i}\cap A_{j}=\emptyset
,
i\neq j
)
\implies

\biguplus\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}A_{i}\in\mathcal{D}

{prop}

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a Dynkin system, then the following is equivalent:

•

\mathcal{D}
is a
\sigma
-algebra,

•

\mathcal{D}
is
\cap
-stable, i.e. for
A,B\in\mathcal{D}
it follows
A\cap B\in\mathcal{D}
.

{defi}

Let
\mathcal{E}\subset\mathcal{P}(\Omega)
be a non-empty family of subsets of
\Omega
. Then we define

\sigma(\mathcal{E})=\bigcap\limits_{\mathcal{E}\subset B,\ B\ \sigma-\text{algebra}}B

as the
\sigma
-Algebra generated by
\mathcal{E}
. Similarly we define

d(\mathcal{E})=\bigcap\limits_{\mathcal{E}\subset B,\ B\ \text{Dynkin system}}B

as the Dynkin system generated by
\mathcal{E}
.

{thm}

Let
\mathcal{E}\subset\mathcal{P}(\Omega)
be a non-empty family of subsets of
\Omega
which is
\cap
-stable, then
d(\mathcal{E})=\sigma(\mathcal{E})
.

Remark
\thethm
.

In order to prove that some abstract condition
\oplus
is satisfied for all
A\in\mathcal{A}
, where
\mathcal{A}
is a
\sigma
-algebra over
\Omega
, we can follow a specific strategy:

1.

define the set
\mathcal{M}=\{A\in\mathcal{A}\mid\text{condition}\ \oplus\ \text{is satisfied for}\ A\}\subset\mathcal{A}
,

2.

prove that
\mathcal{M}
is a Dynkin system,

3.

find a
\cap
-stable generator
\mathcal{E}
of
\mathcal{A}
such that
\mathcal{E}\subset\mathcal{M}
,

4.

imply that

\mathcal{A}=\sigma(\mathcal{E})=d(\mathcal{E})\overset{\mathcal{E}\subseteq\mathcal{M}}{\subseteq}d(\mathcal{M})\overset{\mathcal{M}\ \text{Dynkin system}}{=}\mathcal{M}\subseteq\mathcal{A},

which yields that
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{A}
, i.e. condition
\oplus
is satisfied for all
A\in\mathcal{A}
.

A.5
Martingales

In this section, we briefly recall Doob’s martingale convergence theorem, in particular for supermartingales. We refer to
[
26
, Section 9–11]
for more details.

{defi}

Let
(\Omega,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
be a filtered probability space. We call stochastic process
X=(X_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}

martingale
with respect to the filtration
\mathcal{F}
, if

1.

X
is
\mathcal{F}
-adapted,

2.

\mathbb{E}[|X_{k}|]<\infty
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

3.

\mathbb{E}[X_{k}\mid\mathcal{F}_{l}]=X_{l}
for all
k,l\in\mathbb{N}
with
l\leq k
.

If 3. holds with
\leq
(
\geq
), then we call
X
a
supermartingale
(
submartingale
).

{thm}

[Doob’s supermartingale convergence]
Let
X=(X_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be a supermartingale or submartingale with

\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\ \mathbb{E}[|X_{k}|]<\infty,

then
(X_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
converges almost surely to an
\mathcal{F}_{\infty}
-measurable and integrable random variable
X_{\infty}
.

Remark
\thethm
.

For a martingale we have the property of constant expectation
\mathbb{E}[X_{k}]=\mathbb{E}[X_{0}]
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
. For a supermartingale in contrast, we have decreasing expectation
\mathbb{E}[X_{k}]\leq\mathbb{E}[X_{l}]\leq\mathbb{E}[X_{0}]
for
k\geq l
. Therefore, it is sufficient to replace the condition
\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\ \mathbb{E}[|X_{k}|]<\infty
of Theorem
A.5
with the expectation of the negative part
X_{k}^{-}=\max(0,-X_{k})
, since we have

\mathbb{E}[|X_{k}|]=\mathbb{E}[X_{k}^{+}+X_{k}^{-}]=\mathbb{E}[X_{k}^{+}-X_{k}^{-}+X_{k}^{-}+X_{k}^{-}]=\mathbb{E}[X_{k}]+2\mathbb{E}[X_{k}^{-}]\leq\mathbb{E}[X_{0}]+2\mathbb{E}[X_{k}^{-}].

In case that
X_{k}
is bounded from below it follows that
\mathbb{E}[X_{k}^{-}]<\infty
. This means it is sufficient to prove a uniform lower bound on
(X_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
in order to apply Theorem
A.5
.

A.6
Deferred proofs

A.6.1
Deferred proofs of Chapter
2

Proof of Theorem
2.2.2
.

We will prove the assertion via contradiction. Firstly, suppose that
\bar{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is an accumulating point of the sequence
(x_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfying
\nabla f(\bar{x})\neq 0
. Define the corresponding sub-sequence
(x_{k_{n}})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converging to
\bar{x}
. Since the objective function
f
is continuous, the sequence
(f(x_{k_{n}}))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to
f(\bar{x})
. Moreover, through the Armijo step size rule it follows that the sequence
(f(x_{k}))_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is monotonically decreasing, i.e.
f(x_{k+1})<f(x_{k})
, and hence, the whole sequence
(f(x_{k}))_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to
f(\bar{x})
. In particular, it follows that
(f(x_{k}))_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is a Cauchy-sequence and therefore

\lim_{k\to\infty}(f(x_{k+1})-f(x_{k}))=0.

Due to the Armijo step size rule,

f(x_{k})-f(x_{k+1})\geq\sigma\alpha_{k}\|\nabla f(x_{k})\|\,,

implying that

\lim_{k\to\infty}\alpha_{k}\|\nabla f(x_{k})\|=0.

Since we have assumed that
\nabla f(\bar{x})\neq 0
it follows from continuity of
\nabla f
that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\|\nabla f(x_{k_{n}})\|\neq 0
, and hence,
\lim_{n\to\infty}\alpha_{k_{n}}=0
. By construction of the Armijo step size rule, we can write
\alpha_{k_{n}}=\rho^{\ell_{n}}\cdot s_{0}
, where
\ell_{n}\in\mathbb{N}
denotes the first iteration such that condition (
2.7
) is satisfied. Since
\lim_{n\to\infty}\alpha_{k_{n}}=0
, for
n\in\mathbb{N}
large enough there exists
\ell_{n}>0
such that
f(x_{k_{n}}+\rho^{\ell_{n}-1}s_{0}d_{k_{n}})>f(x_{k_{n}})+\sigma\rho^{\ell_{n}-1}s_{0}\nabla f(x_{k_{n}})^{\top}d_{k_{n}}
, which means that condition (
2.7
) is at least once not satisfied during the application of Algorithm
2
. Thus, we have

\frac{f(x_{k_{n}}+\rho^{\ell_{n}-1}s_{0}d_{k_{n}})-f(x_{k_{n}})}{\rho^{\ell_{n}-1}s_{0}}>\sigma\nabla f(x_{k_{n}})^{\top}d_{k_{n}}.

(A.5)

With the mean-value theorem there exists some
r_{n}\in[0,\rho^{\ell_{n}-1}\cdot s_{0}]
such that

\displaystyle f(x_{k_{n}}+\rho^{\ell_{n}-1}s_{0}d_{k_{n}})-f(x_{k_{n}})

\displaystyle=\nabla f(x_{k_{n}}+r_{n}d_{k_{n}})^{\top}(x_{k_{n}}+\rho^{\ell_{n}-1}s_{0}d_{k_{n}}-x_{k_{n}})

\displaystyle=\rho^{\ell_{n}-1}s_{0}\nabla f(x_{k_{n}}+r_{n}d_{k_{n}})^{\top}d_{k_{n}},

and with (
A.5
) it follows

\nabla f(x_{k_{n}}+r_{n}d_{k_{n}})^{\top}d_{k_{n}}>\sigma\nabla f(x_{k_{n}})^{\top}d_{k_{n}}.

(A.6)

Due to continuity of
\nabla f
and the assumption that
\lim_{n\to\infty}x_{k_{n}}=\bar{x}
, we obtain

\lim_{n\to\infty}d_{k_{n}}=\lim_{n\to\infty}-\frac{\nabla f(x_{k_{n}})}{\|\nabla f(x_{k_{n}})\|}=-\frac{\nabla f(\bar{x})}{\|\nabla f(\bar{x})\|},

and, since
\lim_{n\to\infty}\alpha_{k_{n}}=0
, we obtain

\lim_{n\to\infty}r_{n}=\rho^{\ell_{n}-1}s_{0}=\frac{\rho^{\ell_{n}}}{\rho}s_{0}=\frac{1}{\rho}\alpha_{k_{n}}=0.

Both limits together imply that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\nabla f(x_{k_{n}}+r_{n}d_{k_{n}})^{\top}d_{k_{n}}=-\frac{\nabla f(\bar{x})^{\top}\nabla f(\bar{x})}{\|\nabla f(\bar{x})\|}=-\|\nabla f(\bar{x})\|.

and similarly,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\sigma\nabla f(x_{k_{n}})^{\top}d_{k_{n}}=-\sigma\|\nabla f(\bar{x})\|.

Finally, taking the limit
n\to\infty
in equation (
A.6
) it follows

-\|\nabla f(\bar{x})\|\geq-\sigma\|\nabla f(\bar{x})\|,

which contradicts the assumption
\sigma\in(0,1)
for
\nabla f(\bar{x})\neq 0
. This proves that
\nabla f(\bar{x})=0
.
∎

A.6.2
Deferred proofs of Chapter
3

Proof of Lemma
3.3.1
.

Firstly, we write down the increments of
(E_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}

E_{k+1}-E_{k}=\frac{1}{2}\|z_{k+1}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\|z_{k}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}+A_{k+1}(f(y_{k+1})-f^{\ast})-A_{k}(f(y_{k})-f^{\ast})

and observe that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\|z_{k+1}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\|z_{k}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\|z_{k+1}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\|(z_{k}-z_{k+1})+(z_{k+1}-x_{\ast})\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\|z_{k+1}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\|z_{k}-z_{k+1}\|^{2}-\langle z_{k}-z_{k+1},z_{k+1}-x_{\ast}\rangle-\frac{1}{2}\|z_{k+1}-x_{\ast}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\|z_{k}-z_{k+1}\|^{2}-\langle z_{k}-z_{k+1},z_{k+1}-x_{\ast}\rangle=-\frac{1}{2}\|z_{k}-z_{k+1}\|^{2}+\langle x_{\ast}-z_{k+1},(A_{k+1}-A_{k})\nabla f(x_{k})\rangle.

This leads to

\displaystyle E_{k+1}-E_{k}

\displaystyle=\langle x_{\ast}-z_{k+1},(A_{k+1}-A_{k})\nabla f(x_{k})\rangle-\frac{1}{2}\|z_{k}-z_{k+1}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+A_{k+1}(f(y_{k+1})-f^{\ast})-A_{k}(f(y_{k})-f^{\ast})

\displaystyle=\langle x_{\ast}-z_{k},(A_{k+1}-A_{k})\nabla f(x_{k})\rangle+\langle z_{k}-z_{k+1},(A_{k+1}-A_{k})\nabla f(x_{k})\rangle-\frac{1}{2}\|z_{k}-z_{k+1}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+A_{k+1}(f(y_{k+1})-f^{\ast})-A_{k}(f(y_{k})-f^{\ast}).

Since
0\leq\frac{1}{2}\|a-b\|^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\|a\|^{2}-\langle a,b\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\|b\|^{2}
for
a,b\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we can apply the inequality
\langle a,b\rangle-\frac{1}{2}\|a\|^{2}\leq\frac{1}{2}\|b\|^{2}
to derive

\langle z_{k}-z_{k+1},(A_{k+1}-A_{k})\nabla f(x_{k})\rangle-\frac{1}{2}\|z_{k}-z_{k+1}\|^{2}\leq\frac{1}{2}\|(A_{k+1}-A_{k})\nabla f(x_{k})\|^{2}

such that

\displaystyle E_{k+1}-E_{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(A_{k+1}-A_{k})^{2}\|\nabla f(x_{k})\|^{2}+\langle x_{\ast}-z_{k},(A_{k+1}-A_{k})\nabla f(x_{k})\rangle

\displaystyle\quad+A_{k+1}(f(y_{k+1})-f^{\ast})-A_{k}(f(y_{k})-f^{\ast}).

Moreover, we observe that

\displaystyle A_{k+1}(f(y_{k+1})-f^{\ast})-A_{k}(f(y_{k})-f^{\ast})

\displaystyle=(A_{k+1}-A_{k})(f(x_{k})-f^{\ast})+A_{k}(f(x_{k})-f(y_{k}))

\displaystyle\quad+A_{k+1}(f(y_{k+1}-f(x_{k})).

With
\varepsilon_{k+1}:=\frac{1}{2}(A_{k+1}-A_{k})^{2}\|\nabla f(x_{k})\|^{2}+A_{k+1}(f(y_{k+1})-f(x_{k}))
it follows that

\displaystyle E_{k+1}-E_{k}

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon_{k+1}+\langle x_{\ast}-z_{k},(A_{k+1}-A_{k})\nabla f(x_{k})\rangle

\displaystyle\quad+(A_{k+1}-A_{k})(f(x_{k})-f^{\ast})+A_{k}(f(x_{k})-f(y_{k})),

and it is left to prove that

\mathcal{R}=\langle x_{\ast}-z_{k},(A_{k+1}-A_{k})\nabla f(x_{k})\rangle+(A_{k+1}-A_{k})(f(x_{k})-f^{\ast})+A_{k}(f(x_{k})-f(y_{k}))\leq 0.

We add
-y_{k}+y_{k}=0
to derive

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}

\displaystyle=\langle x_{\ast}-y_{k},(A_{k+1}-A_{k})\nabla f(x_{k})\rangle+\langle y_{k}-z_{k},(A_{k+1}-A_{k})\nabla f(x_{k})\rangle

\displaystyle\quad+(A_{k+1}-A_{k})(f(x_{k})-f^{\ast})+A_{k}(f(x_{k})-f(y_{k})),

where we now aim to apply convexity of
f
in the form of

f(z)-f(y)+\langle y-z,\nabla f(z)\rangle\leq 0.

Therefore, using
y_{k}-z_{k}=\frac{A_{k+1}}{A_{k+1}-A_{k}}(y_{k}-x_{k})
we again rewrite
\mathcal{R}
in the form of

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}

\displaystyle=\langle x_{\ast}-y_{k},(A_{k+1}-A_{k})\nabla f(x_{k})\rangle+A_{k+1}\langle y_{k}-x_{k},\nabla f(x_{k})\rangle

\displaystyle\quad+(A_{k+1}-A_{k})(f(x_{k})-f^{\ast})+A_{k}(f(x_{k})-f(y_{k}))

\displaystyle=\langle x_{\ast}-y_{k},(A_{k+1}-A_{k})\nabla f(x_{k})\rangle+A_{k+1}\langle y_{k}-x_{k},\nabla f(x_{k})\rangle

\displaystyle\quad-A_{k}\langle x_{k},\nabla f(x_{k})\rangle+A_{k}\langle x_{k},\nabla f(x_{k})\rangle

\displaystyle\quad+(A_{k+1}-A_{k})(f(x_{k})-f^{\ast})+A_{k}(f(x_{k})-f(y_{k}))

\displaystyle=(A_{k+1}-A_{k})\langle x_{\ast}-x_{k},\nabla f(x_{k})\rangle+A_{k}\langle y_{k}-x_{k},\nabla f(x_{k})\rangle

\displaystyle\quad+(A_{k+1}-A_{k})(f(x_{k})-f^{\ast})+A_{k}(f(x_{k})-f(y_{k}))

\displaystyle=(A_{k+1}-A_{k})\left\{f(x_{k})-f(x_{\ast})+\langle x_{\ast}-x_{k},\nabla f(x_{k})\rangle\right\}

\displaystyle\quad+A_{k}\left\{f(x_{k})-f(y_{k})+\langle y_{k}-x_{k},\nabla f(x_{k})\rangle\right\}

\displaystyle\leq 0

by convexity of
f
. We have proved that

E_{k+1}-E_{k}\leq\varepsilon_{k+1}
.
∎
